Atter had any Kiva or an understanding existing between his carter and his daughter he would be dismissed at once. But, he reasoned, he was quite as lit a match for Nelly as the miller was when proposed and was accepted by the daughter of the Hall Farm steward. His father, though he was only a wherryman, owned the wherry he sailed in and had another vessel beside, and lie had taken sonic trouble over Joe's "schooling," seeing that he did not start in life entirely unequipped for the struggle. Why should he not tell Nelly all he had say ? or as much of it as he found possible; why should he not let her know why it was he wanted to go to a larger town and save money ? If she would have nothing to do with him—well, he would go away all the same, hut if she cared at all for him, and could give him any hope that one day he might coin and claim her, would not that know- ledge spur nim on in his efforts to provide a honie for her. So he said: ; "And then, Nelly, I should ask you to share what- ever good luck was mine. I know what your father would say—l've thought about all that, and made u my mind not to heed it. I love you very much, Nelly—better than I can tell you. Do you care for me at all ? Do you love Ane even a little ? Vili you wait a little time while I work hard and make a home for you ?" _

Nelly had to some extent -conquered her shyness now, and looked frankly into her lover's face as he bent anxiously over her. Never before, she thought, had he appea,yed so brave and manly as with that look of earnest determination on his face. Like Jos, she could form a pretty correct idea of what her father's feelings would be in the matter; she could well imagine his indignation if he knew that one of his men, whom he paid so much ,a week to do as he was told, had dared to speak thus to his daughter. But surely, she thought, it was worth while risking such disp!easure to enjoy such love as Joe could give her. She had no diubt whatever that Joe meant all he said; she could read the truth of his eager, earnest declaration in his eyes, but, womanlike, she did not answer at once. "Are you sure, Joe," she said, and she smiled up at him as she spoke, "are you quite sure you will not change your mind if you go away from Holworth ? You will 'cro into large towns, and wonderful places very different from this quiet place. Suppose you go down to the coast and find work there, and succeed in it, don't you think you will soon say to yourself, What is the use ? Is it worth while working hard for a girl who lives away at that sleepy Holworth, and who knows nothing of what life is like in towns like this, when there are plenty of girls here—prettier, smarter, cleverer girls—whom I could have for the asking ? ' *rid if you found it harder to get on than you expected. wouldn't you think you had mad a mistake, and wish you had never asked me to wait for you ?" "I would work—l would work day and night—l would wait for years, Nelly; if only you would say you cared a little for me! I will leave here to- morrow—" "To-morrow! Oh, no, Joe! not to-morrow! Stay with me a ,ittle longer !" "Then you do care a little for me, Nelly? If so, hard as it is, the quicker I go the better, for the sooner I shall be able to come back and claim you." He took her hand as he spoke, and d:ew her towards him. Nelly's han I remained in his, and clasped it gently as she sank down on an old, wooden bench that stood by the side of the path. Joe seated him- self by her, but: could hardly see her face, which was sligotiy.turned from him. She did not speak, and he put his arm round her neck and drew her face towards him. Then her eyes- sought his. This time she did not look away, and then their lips met! Old Mall, who passed along the bank, glanced over the fence and caught sight of the two figures on the garden bench. Ile stopped for &moment and chuckled, but neither Joe nor Nelly saw or heard him, and he went his way to his cottage, leaving them in the drt:amland of youth and love. -

On the following morning the miller rose at his usual early hour. After looking round the mid,•which had been kept going all night, he took his stand against the door of his dusty office, and watched Joe and another of his men lowering sacks of flour from the upper floor into a waggon in the yard below. The miller was a stolid-looking, .heavily-built man, whose face bore traces of the hardening in- fluence of a tough struggle for existence. He had mastered his many difficulties by unremitting toil. Never had he allowed any consideration for others to stand in his way, and in his fight for a livelihood he had never felt any remorse at seeing those with whom he was brought into commercial Contact go ter the wall. The death of his wife, which had occurred some three years before, had apparently made as little impression upon him as if the mis- fortune had been another's, and the manner of his daily life was little changed by the narrowing of his home cir:de." Although he seldom quarrelled, or had a rough- word with any of his neighbours, he was little liked or respected by them. • His life was self-centred, hedged about by the circumscribed limits of his own immediate concerns. It was not often that he troubled himself much about Nelly, who, since her mother's death, had taken over the management of his domestic affairs, but he had a kind of jealous regard for her, and had more than once warned off his premises certain love-sick youths, who were attracted by his daughter's pretty face. As to there being any danger of complica- tions lurking in his immediate household, such an idea never dawned upon hit, and consequently Joe had enjoyed a freedom in Nelly's society that was denied to the love-smitten youths of the town. Such being the case it was with considerable sur- prise and indignation that the miller listened to the garrulous remarks of old Allan, who had paused in .passing down the road to have a talk with him. "Mornin''miller," said old Miall. "Suppaise you'll sounbe thinkin' of taking a rest, and lettin' younger heads manage the mill business? You've had a good spell on it yourself, and done very well by it ;iit's only right you should let Others take sonic of the burden, and do some of the hard work." "What are you talking about ?" said the miller, somewhat indifferently, for he frequently found the 'old man's gossip wearisome. "Who said anything about taking a rest ? I've never had any rest yet-, and don't .suppose lever shall." "Hall! hall !miller, that's all very well," chuckled his companion, "but I see'd 'em myself, though they were too busy to notice me. Well, he's a good- loAin' enough young man as far as I'm any judge" —and he looked in the direction of the unsn,peeting Joe—"and 'ull-make a steady,' hard-workin' son-in- law. But perhaps you mean to keep an eye on 'em yourself for a time, just to see that things go right, oh?" "What the blazes are you talking about, you old fool ?" burst out the miller. "What's he got to do with being a 'son-in-law of mine? Like enough I should think of marryin' my girl to a young water- rat, who's spent all his time lazying up and down the river in a wherry! I should just like to see him hanging round my daughter, that's all. I'd soon let him know his place, and send him about his business."

"Oh, no offence, miller, no offence. But one can't help seem' things, you know, and if you hain't noticed it yourself, you'd better keep your eyes open, that's all. I don't want to interfere—tain't no concern o' mine—but, it strikes me that if you don't keep a sharp look-out the young uns'll get the better on you.' "What do you mean, blame you?" shouted the miller, whose indignation was rapidly getting the better of him. "If you've seen anything, say so ;' don't keep marndering round the Matter, and blink- ing like a cat in the sun." 'Old Miall was no friend of the miller's, and by no means desirous of obliging him now that he found be was ignorant o ~what had really taken Place, but the opportunity- of telling him such an interesting piece of news, and watching his tempestuous indignation, was too good to lose. He, therefore, with many grins And winks, recounted what be had witnessed on the previous evening, and was amply rewarded by seeing the miller become almost beside himself with rage. When he had finished his story, which he told with considerable relish, the miller. with a parting curse, flung himself into his office, and, slamming the door behind him, passed through into the house in search of his daughter. -

After an absence of about half-an-hour he re- appeared, awl, seeing Joe at work in the granary, told him to "take himself off" at once. Joe needed no explanation to account for his sudden dismissal; he could read the cause of it in the miller's flushed face and Euppressed anger. He, therefore, put on his coat, and left the mill quietly, feeling that it was his wisest course not to say anything to increase the miller's resentment.

He did not, however, wander far from the mill during the day, and when the evening came on he sat down by the riverside within sight of the mill- Inmse door, hoping that, perhaps, Nelly might see him, and come out to him. Be was not surprised, however, when he found it had grown dark without his seeing any sign of her, as he guessed the•miller would keen a strict watch over her movements. But on the following night the miller was obliged to &INC Off to a neighbouring village on business, and net long after he had left the Mill Joe saw the door open, and Nelly coming towards him along the river batik.

Ile met her at ti stile, which stool between two of the riverside marshes, and would not lot her say a word until he had blamed himself for the trouble that had come upon her. But NeHy seemed to have little ?bought for herself ; all she appear( I to realise was that Joe had, for her sake, been turned away by her father, and with a• woman's su•eptibility she took upon herself all the blame. But this Joe would not hear of, and he tried to cheer her up by tc]ling her that what had occurred could not make very much difference to them, as they had already

arruinvl tint he should g.o awa.e after a week or two to look Out for work at the port: What her father lia_d said to her, Nelly would not repeat, and Joe rilthtly guessed that she was site,lt in this res-twet for fear of hurtin2- his feeliwzs., He kaew the miller

BY THE no MILL STREAM.

DY MARC US DEANE.

Author of "Harold 'AlaiTiott," "Only a Year Ago," !../7.

It was the time of the annual weed-cutting, and old Miall, who was seated on an antiquated milking sto•'l, angling for perch at a bend of the stream, muttered frequent imprecations as the matted clumps of weeds came floating down, and compelled him every few minutes to haul in his line. Bolting parties from the neighbouring town had the gi.ut-st difficulty in forcing their craft through the masses of tangled vegetation which blocked up the narrovl reaches of the river, and the ducks and geese wen greatly disconcerted ,by the large patches which cam/ sailing along and bore them down the stream. Higher up, where the river swept round till tnar-shy borders of a wide -ostretch..of common-land, miller's weed-cutters were still at work, though the day was drawing to a close. Their method of clearing the river was a very Apple one, such as had been taught, them by therr marshland fore- fathers, but it was effectual, and the idea of intro- ducing any more mOdern plan never occurred to them. They used a number of old scythe-blades, riveted loosely together. To each end of this formidable chain a rope Was fa, toned, by means of which it was dragged along the bed of the stream, and worked backwards and forwards from bank to bank by the men, who thus slowly made their way up the narrow river. Joe Harper was sitting in an old gun-punt about a hundred yards below the spot where the cutters were at work. It was. his duty to follow them up the , .stream, in order to free the scythes, or knives as they were called, from any obstacles they might encounter in the river bed. Now, however, his task was done, and in a few minutes the cutters would have finished their work for the day. Joe had been in the miller's employ but a few months, and this was the first time he had taken any part in the cutting of the weeds. Ever since .his schooldays had ended, and until the previous spring, he had been employed on board one of the wherries which sailed lazily between the sleepy inland towns and the busy seaport at the mouth of the river. He had, however, grown tired of .river life, and determined, if only for a time, to settle down at Holworth,a quiet little place some eighteen or twenty miles from, the coast. He had no difficulty in finding employment, for he was a -steady,industrious young fellow, who could be depended upon and was not afraid of work. Now, however, he was for the second time wondering if the life he had chosen was the best for him; but it was for a different reason than on the former occasion that he now felt unsettled and undecided as to what he should do. His uncertainty was easy to account for. He had not long been in the employ of the miller before he felt that a new influence had been brought to bear on his life, and one that was by no means untileas- ing to him. This influence .was exerted by the miller's daughter, Nelly, who had proved herself of a very friendly disposition, and by no means averse to an occasional chat with the new-corner. Joe was a good-looking fellow,, and during his river life had seen enough outside the little town of Holworth to make him an interesting companion. Such being the case, it was hardly surprising that an acquaint- ance, which rapidly turned to friendship, sprang up between them, attracting little attention in the sleepy town where desirable friends and acquaint- ances were so few. But, though at present there were few signs of its development in Nelly, there soon manifested itself in Joe a very perceptible inclination towards the fair-haired, winsome girl; and, as the days passed and their friendship deepened and strengthened, lie became more and more unsettled. Again and again he asked himself whether it was to his best interests to remain a miller's hand when he might find better and more remunerative work at 'the neighbouring port. And it was not wholly for his own sake that he desired to "better himself," but in order that he might some day—how remote he did not ask him- self—come to Nelly and open his heart-to:her, and approach her father with a confidence that was now impossible. By-and-bye the weed-cutters dragged the scythes from the water and left off .work for the day, but Joe still remained in the boat, wlrich was moored to the bank, and, resting his head on his hands, 'gazed away across the meadows and along the side of the

stream. It was a warm, still evening. Over in the west I the sky was tinged with the ruddy glow of the sinking sun, which had now almost disappeared below the horizon. The note of the night-jar could now and again be heard from a clump of trees by the river, and the harsh cry of the corn-crake came mournfully from the long grass of the marshland. A water rat rustled among the sedges, and here and there a dace gave a splash in the stream. But for these 'familiar and unheeded sounds- there was a brooding silence over •tho wide-spreading marshes. Presently Joe bestirred himself, and standing up looked away towards the mill, which, though quite three-quarters of an hour's row down the 'stream, was but a short way off, as the crow flies. He wondered if Nelly would come along by the river bank that evening,. as she had done on several occasions during the weed-cutting in order to watch the men at work. Once wile!) she had done so Joe had rowed her home, and since that evening he had kept the boat dry and free from weeds in .the hope of another such pleasant task. To row Nelly, down the river on a summer evening, when the lingering sunlight fell on her pretty face and laughing eyes, was a task that many would have sought with eagerness, and See appreciated it at its full worth. But to-night there seemed little prospect of any such welcome duty ,falling to his lot, and, after a last look along the bank he was about to unmoor the boat, when a voice, which he recognised with a thrill of pleasure, called to him from a little higher up the stream. It was Nelly's, and turning round Joe saw her coming towards the river side from an opposite direction to that in which he had looked for her. "Joe," she called to him, and it sounded very pleasant to hear his Christian name used by so pretty a girl as Nelly, "Joe, I am going home. Can you row me, or are 'you to) tired ?". "Come to the old boathouse steps," said Joe, by way of an answer, and seating himself in the boat, with a few vigorous strokes he brought it alongside. the spot indicated. As he took Nelly's hand to help her into the boat, for the second time he experienced a strange thrill of joy, and he was in no hurry to relinquish it until she was safely seated. As she placed her foot on the side of the punt and sprang lightly towards him, he could not but notice with admiration the perfect outlines of her figure, and the natural grace of her every pose and movement. "What were you looking for as I came across the meadow ?" asked the girl, when she had taken the rudder-lines, and they were slowly gliding down the stream. "I was watching you, and you stood - up three or four times after I crossed the bridge." As she spoke she glanced curiously at Joe, who, however, looked straight into her face as he replied: "I was looking for you. I thought perhaps you ;night come to sae the men at work as you _did on Tuesday." . Nelly turned her face away at this direct and unhesitating reply, and hastened to explain that she had been to the town on an errand for her father, and as the evening was so fine had taken the longer way home, round by the common and across the bridge. As she .made this somewhat unnecessary explanation she glanced round the boat, and did not fail to notice that it had recently been baled out and freed of its customary bunches of wet weeds. Somehow it pleased her to think that Joe had taken the trouble to do this, in spite of the uncertainty as to whether he would be allowed to row her home again. A few words were said about the weed-cutting, but neither Joe nor Nelly seemed much inclined for talking, and the boat slowly rounded' bend after bend of the river until it reached the miller's boat- house, which stood at the bottom of the mill-house garden. When Joe had assisted Nelly ashore, he fastened the punt to a stake—the boat-house being little used in the summer time—and followed her up the path. Having overtaken her, he turned towards her and said: "I am glad you let me row you home to-night. I am going away soon:"' Nelly's heart gave a throb; though why. she could hardly understand, and she stopped and looked at Joe with a shy glance. Then, somewhat tremulously and hesitatinaly, she said: "Going away, Joe ! Why ?" Joe's eyes met hers as she glanced up at him, and he kept them fixed upon her until she felt the colour come with aso ;den flush to her cheeks. She turned her head away, and plucked a blossom from a bush by her side ; pulled it to pieces, and scattered the leaves on the path, but was afraid to meet his searching look again. "I- can't stop here," replied Joe, after a pause, "I must go away to the coast. Jack Waller, who came up in the Susie, says there's plenty of work to he had at the port, and that I should do a lot better there than here. Net that I ain't comfortable here," he continued, hastily, "but I can't stop, Nelly " he had never called' her "Nelly " hel'ore, but she said nothing, aad did not even raise her es- a. •` Perhaps, after ati u, if I do well, I may come back, and then—a "And then what— Jo. ?" She did not look up, and her voice trembled as she put the question.. Then Joe made up his mind to make a plunge, and tall her outright that Ile loved her. After all, why should he not do so ? He knew well enough that he had no chance With the miller, and that if the

For a long time they stood by the stile, talking as only lovers will, and finding comfort in each other's presence, and when it became late, and Nelly knew she must return to the mill, she was in far better spirits than when she stole out to her clandestine meeting. She admired Joe the more for the brave way in which he faced his difficulties, and deter- mined that, whatever the future might have in store for them, nothing should shake her allegiance to him. On two or three occasions during the week that elapsed before Joe went away he succeeded in obtainift a few minutes with Nelly. When the last moment came, and they 'knew they must rart—for how long, neither could say—each felt, in the grow- ing strength of their love, better able to meet the future, the one to labour for the end he had in view, the other to wait in patience till her father's dis- pleasure should have passed away, or her lover should have attained a position that would warrant him with confidence to claim her.

When the miller dismissed Joe, and commanded his daughter to have nothing more to do with him, he was satisfied that he had removed all immediate danger. But still he did not feel so sure of his influence as when he had to deal with the verdant youths of Holworth. He had never quite fathomed Nelly; her manner, as she went about the house, was quiet and reserved, and he could form little idea of her thoughts and inclinations. She was in many respects like her mother' only he could not so easily obtain his own way with Nelly as he had with his wife, when she was alive. After giving the matter a good deal of laboured consideration, he came to the conclusion that the best thing he could do was to get Nelly safely and satisfactorily married to someone who fulfilled his ideal of a worthy husband, and who would cure her of her infatuation for Joe. His ideas of what went to make a satisfactory husband, however, were not of a nature to awaken much warmth or interest on the part of his daughter. The man he selected in his own mind as a suit- able son-in-law was the son of a builder, whose premises were situated about half a mile from the mill. His name was Baldry, and his personality and prospects appeared to the miller all that a young girl could desire. He was a steady, business- like young man, with whose character the miller believed no one could find any fault, and many girls in the town would have accepted him without hesitation. But his nature was a good deal like that of the miller—hard and grasning—and Nelly, who could fully apprqciate what a rife led in the com- pany of such a man would mean to her, by no means welcomed the attentions her father en- couraged. It very soon became evident to her what was meant when, with increasing frequency, young Baldry dropped in during the evening, under the pretence of seeing her father about some business, and she was not long in discovering that his visits were Paid with the miller's consent. At first these calls were of a somewhat formal character, and she had little cause for disquietude. After a time, however, it dawned upon the. miller that matters were not progressing so quickly and pleasantly as he would like to see them, and he began to introduce into the conversation laboured witticisms and unwelcome hints, which Nelly received in silence and the young man with an uncomfortable smile.

Befere very long the miller took the opportunity of leaving his daughter and her admirer in the room by themselves, and Baldry, who had been previously acquainted with his intention, broached the subject which, presumably, was nearest his heart. But he found no encouragement forthcoming from Nelly, who steadily gave him to understand that his attentions were distasteful to her. In spite of this rebuff, however,- he did not desist from calling at the house, but kept up his periodical visits, in the hope that he might in time either make a more favourable impression upon her or weary her into consenting to his suit. He cared for her as much as it was possible for him to care for anyone, but still he would not have persisted in his addresses but for the substantial benefit to be deriveill from a union with the miller's only child. Meanwhile, Nelly was cheered from time to time : by hearing that Joe had been successful in the new sphere to which he had transferred himself. Before leaving Holworth he had made arrangements with the landlady with whom be had lodged—the widow of an old wherryman, with whom he was a great favourite—that she should receive any letters he sent for Nelly, for he feared that otherwise they would be kept from her by the miller. By this means Nelly -learnt that, shortly after his arrival at the port, he had found employment with a smack- owner, who had known his father, and who gave him the superintendence of the stores used in his trawling vessels. In a later letter Joe sent the welcome news that he hoped shortly to be promoted to the more responsible position of agent for the smackowner, who was removing to a: neighbouring port, from which several of his trawlers sailed. These letters Nelly treasured up, and read over and over again irf the quiet of her bedroom; and their contents strengthened her in her determination not to give way to the double pressure brought to bear upon her by her father and her troublesome suitor. Still, it was a wearying time for her, and but for the hope that it would not be very long before Joe would be able to come and claim her, she would often have given way to despairing conjectures as to what was to be the end of it all. Her father was growing irritable towards her, and frequently expressed his dissatisfaction at the coolness of her treatment of her importunate admirer. As to the latter, in spite of Nelly's evident disinclination for his society, he continued to call at the mill, and often, when she was out walkin;.-f, would join her, and refuse to tale any notice of her hints that she preferred to be alone. In consequence of these marked attentions it was soon generally agreed in the little town that the miller's daughter had at last found a lover to her taste. When:once this idea became rooted in the minds of the town gossips it not easily displaced, and, naturally enough, before long a rumour of the sup- posed engagement reached Joe's ears. At first he merely smiled when he heard of the visits of the young builder to the mill-house, for he did not look upon Baldry as at all a formidable rival, but, by- and-bye, he heard that Nelly was in the habit of taking walks with her new admirer, and this made him somewhat uncomfortable. Still, he continued : to receive Nelly's letters, and, with such evidence of her love as they contained before him, he could not but feel sure of her faithfulness to him. At last, however, the news came up by one of the wherries that Nelly was going :to be married to her new suitor, and, though in his heart he felt there could : be no truth in the story, he decided to pay a visit to HolWorth, in order to set, his mind quite at ease. .As Christmas was approaching, and the smack- : owner's:vessels were all in port for the holidays, he was able:to get away without difficulty, and started for the little inland town full of pleasant anticipa- tion at the thought that in a few hours he would see : his Nelly, awl hear again her sweet assurances of love.

That Christmas the Frost King reigned supreme over the Broadland. As was frequently the case in winter, the river had overflowed the low, level marshes, and they were transformed into wide- stretching sheets of ice, so clear that the grass beneath could be seen is through a covering of glass. All along the intersecting roadways the stunted trees were outlined with the powdery white dust of the frozen night mists, the gates and railings by the side of the raised'pathS glistened as though sprinkling with diamonds. For some days the skaters had been holding high revel on the meadows, and now the ice on the river itself was so thick that some of the more venturesome of their number had essayed, upon its frozen surface. Still the majority of the skaters preferred the marshee, for the river ice had been broken up to allow of the passage of a wherry, and abounded in treacherous spots. The reach above the mill, however, was considered quite safe, and emongst those who betook themselves there. for an hour or two's enjoyment was Nelly, who, . like nearly all the marshland girls, was an adept at skating. She had had her skates on but a very few minutes; however, before she perceived, to her annoyance, that young Baldry had also selected that particular reach for an afternoon's recreation. It was not long before he caught sight of her, and he at once came up and skated by her side down.the river. Hoping he would soon leave her, Nelly skated sl9wly along, and saki as little as possible in reply to the complimentary speeches with which he attempted to justify his unwelcome attentions. But it was soon evident that he was in no hurry to leave her, and presently he seized her han s, and exclaimed: "Come along Nell ; let's have a spin together as far. as the wooden bridge." Much as site would have liked to do so, the girl found it impossible to get away from hint, and she was, per- force, obliged to let him have his own way. Just at this moment Joe came strolling along the hank of the frozen river. He had arrived at Holworth an hour or two before, and had ascertained from his old landlady that; she bad seen Nelly walk- ing towards the river carrying her skates -in her hands. When he caught sight of his sweetheart and her companion, he was pained and startled to find them apparently on such good teeny; with each other. It seemed to him such an evident confirmation of all the unpleasant rumours that had reached his cars, that in another in meta he was overwhelmed by a flood of an re H,e., :;-os and leter feeling agamet the girl had so heartlessly deceived him. -s] ]]]],,, \'].a -Baldry notk!ed him as he stood - them the bank, and it struck o '• er • far too pleasantly occupi 0;1 eo t,:e regard to anyone but

river, and walked in the direction of the milt. As• to the man who appeared to have supplanted him, Joe hardly gave him a thought. He had known him when he lived at Holworth, and failed to see what t.law was in him that could Dossibly attract such a girl as Nelly; but that was her affair. It was apt cool, thouqhtless. manner in which she seemed tl have throNlh him over that angered him, and Maths him say hitter things to himself as he walked along_ ") doubt," he thought, "she is deceiving us both and believes that neither knows of her relations with the other. Well, if that is the sort of girl She is, she may go her way. I've done with her." But soon he began to think less harshly of the girl, for he could not deny to himself that he still loved her dearly. True, he had thought her faithful to him, and that she was willing to make some: sacrifice in order to marry him; but now, no •doubt,. 1:1),!, had changed her mind, and realised that it wasi no little thing to give up the position of comparative ,!ase and comfort which was offered her and risk: the hardships that might attend a humble marriage. He was blind, he told himself, not to have scow: before how likely it was that somfthing of the kind, might happen; and now that it had occurred, was it so very surprising after all. By this time Joe had almost reached the mill, and' Was about to turn the bend of the road leading to' the bridge when lie heard a loud cry froAt down the river.

He stopped short and looked back in the direction. of the frozen reach. As he did so he heard another cry, followed by shouting. and saw several of the skaters hurrying towards the sluice use!' to draw off the overflow of water at time of flood. He knew that at such a spot the ice was almost sure to be unsafe, amid guessed immediokely that an accident must have happened. Immediately all his troubles were dismissed from his mind, and he started running towards the sluice. • As he sped along the bank he caught sight of two or three men dragging a gate, which had been hurriedly lifted from its hinges, across the ice, and he realised at once the nature of the catastrophe. Before the men were halfway across the river he was on the narrow footbridge over the sluice. Almost the first face he noticed amongst the crowd of skaters, who- were gazing horror-struck towards the pool below him, was that of Baldry, white, and wearing a look of utter helplessness. Quickly and anxiously Joe glanced round for some sign of Nelly. She was nowhere to be seen. Then another cry arose, and --Yith excited gestures several persons pointed to a, nat, which Joe at once recognised as Nelly's, float- ing near the edge of a sheet of ice, broken by the opening and'closing of the sluict. gate. In an instant he was on the ice, and rapidly pushed his way through the frighteniid crowd. Quickly but carefully he made his way across the treacherous part, where the ice cracked at every step, and when he found it unsafe to walk further, he laid down and drew himself along until he broken the down, edge. Behind he heard the men pushing the gate towards him, and then a rope, fetched from a neighbouring boat-house, was flung- to him from the foot-bridge. Grasping the rope firmly in his hand, and giving it a twist round his arm, he plunged into the water. Its icy coldness seemed to chill him, to the bone in a'moment, and to take his breath away, but the thought of Nelly, for whom nothing now remained in his heart but love, nerved him to fight against the numbing sensation which crept over 'him. He. rose to the- surface to breathe, and just as he did so caught sight of a hand raised above the water near the edge of the ice. It was visible but for a moment and, . appeared to make a feeble attempt to grasp some- thing; then disappeared again. With a couple of strong strokes Joe reached the spot, and, diving beneath the ice, clutched hold of Nelly's dress. There was no struggle, for the girl had made her last effort, and was now unconscious, and when he drew her towards him, she NN as a dead weight irc his hands. Then a rushing sound came in his ears, and he seemed to fall asleep, and knew no More. _ _

But by this time, two or three of the more daring- of the spectators had followed Joe's example, anct had drawn themselves along the slippery surface of the ice to the edge of the pool. Suddenly the rope they were holding slackened and ceased to move, and after waiting a moment they hauled it in. The unconscious Joe still held the end fast in his grasp, and presently he and his apparently lifeless burden were brought to the surface. With vreat difficu:ty. they were drawn upon the ice, the edge of which: broke two or three times with the weight brought to bear upon it; but at last they were drakged from the chilling boo' to a place of safety. Then: there was no lack of willing hands to assist in every way possible to restore them to consciousness. In a short time Joe was little the worse for his immersion, but for a long while it seemed as. though all attempts to revive Nelly were to be. unavailing. At last, however, a. faint movement Of the heart•was,.detected, followed by a twitching. of the eyelids, and presently she opened her eyes.

It was the morning of Christmas Day, and in the- mill-house parlour Nelly was seated in an armchair dreamily gazing at the flickering firelight. Her face• was rather pale, but there were indications that the colour was returning to her cheeks, while the languor which resulted from her accident on the river was fast leaving her. On the opposite side of the wide, old-fashioned fireplace, the miller sat, looking thoughtfully into his daughter's face. During the past few days a great change had come over the miller • the narrow escape of his young and pretty Nelly had affected him to an extent that he hardly yet realised. When his wife died he had been apparently little moved, and soon resumed his. usual routine manlier of living, as though no place- would for ever be left unfilled in his household. But with Nelly it seemed different; until now ho had not thought it possible that one so young could be taken from him by the remorseless hand that had robbed him of his wife. Now he aPpeared half afraid of his daughter, and when he spoke to her it was with a tenderness that was a surprise to both of them. The shock he had experienced had softened him, and in his heart there was awakening an entirely new feeling towards his only child.. Presently he coughed, and sat up in his chair. "I've asked Joe to look in to-night," he said. "Ii thought maybe you'd like to see him." Nelly's eyes brightened, and a flush came to her- cheeks. Life and hope seemed to come back to.her in a moment. As the tide at flood fills and freshens. the standing pool among the rocks, so a flood of joy filled her heart and renewed her life. "You see," her father continua, hesitatingly, "1 didn't think you really cared for him—thought you didn't know your own mind, and didn't know what you were doing. But I see now I was wrong. At it is, he seems to be doing well at the port, and— well, if you've made up your mind to have Mtn, you can let him know I've no objection." Then the miller coughed again and rose, am], after pausing a moment before the fire, turned. away and left the room, muttering something about business as an explanation of his rapid exit. But Nelly was not long in searching him out, and a few minutes later, as he sat at the desk in his dusty office he felt a hand laid gently on his arm, and a warm kiss on his forehead.

From that hour the miller began to discover that, if he willed it, life in the future might have some- thing better and more beautiful for him than he had ever yet realised.. He commenced to ask himself whether in the past his days had been spent in Fuch a way as would be likely to bring him the most substantial reward; whether, indeed, in grasping at the material 'satisfaction of increasing wealth, ho. had not allowed the More real and true pleasures of home ties and family affection to slip from him. When he felt Nelly's kiss upon his brow it suddenly dawned upon him what a great mistake he had made,, and how much he had unwittingly robbed himself of. And as he bent to kiss, for the first time for. years, that pretty upturned face, a tear trembled for a moment on his eyelid, and then fell on_the .hand resting caressingly on his arm. That evening when Joe called at the mill he found Nelly and her father sitting together by the fire, the- girl with her arm upon the miller's chair, while she iooked up lovingly into the old man's face. Ho. could see in an instant that a great change had taken place in the relations between the father and daughter, and that the conclusions he had drawn when the miller invited him to the house were cor- rect. The latter welcomed him with ,a hearty warmth that made it difficult for him to realise that. the greeting came from the man he had parted ro::te so differently but a few months before. And Nelly. --who in his eyes had never looked so beautiful as now with the flickering firelight playing on her fa-e --her glance o mingled love and happiness assured him that at last all barriers between them had been broken down. That evening seemed to Joe like a dream, and passed as quickly. The miller discreetly withdrew, and left the young.people to themselves. When he returned he found them seated hand in hand, silent but content. It was late when Joe left the mill, 'and as he walked along the snow-whitened road towards his old lodgings he found it hard to lylive that before the coming year was many weeke old a new era in. his life would have opened, and that be and Nelly- woUld be starting forward to meet, the future hand in hand. •

FASHIONS

There would not be much of interest to chronicle this month if it were not for the sales, which, in spite of what some strong- minded individuals may say to the contrary, are ,dear to most feminine hearts. Some, perhaps, of the very greatest bargains may be picked up now, for many tradesmen keep the real bargains to the last, and therefore the closing days of the month often provide the best opportunities. It is a wise woman who yields to the seductions of short lengths of brocade, velvet, satin, lace, or mousseline. There are so many charming and useful details of dress which can be evolved out of two or three yards of any of the fore-mentioned stuffs, such as evening blouses, fronts, sleeves, collars, jabots, and a number of other dainty trifles. The pretty dressing jacket sketched is made of a nice shade of pink flannel trimmed with insertions of valenciennes lace, a bow of soft cream silk gracing the front. This pretty little garment could be made sufficiently dressy to do duty as a tea-jacket by the addition of a lining of white silk.

PRETTY DRESSING JACKET.

The silk to be perfectly cosy for winter wear Ei.hould be wadded and quilted, but if this is thought coo warm or too expensive a lining, the silk could be laid flat on the flannel with or without an interlining of flannelette. Little rosettes of narrow pink 1)61)6 ribbon fixed at the end of the lines of lace insertion look very dainty, and add to the general effect. A dressing jacket takes :up so much less room in a trunk that it appeals strongly to those who pay flying visits to their 'friends. Those who through laziness or weak health frequently breakfast in bed will find the value of a cosy and ornamental little garment such as this which can so easily be slipped on and off, and is not overburdened with a quantity of fussy details such as lace or silk frills, 01 flounces which have the irritating peculiarity of dipping into one's tea cup, or the contents of one's plate. Of course, there is nothing, when out of bed, that affords one such a sense of complete comfort and satisfaction as a good dressing gown, and things have greatly advanced from the days when these "wrappers," as they were called, were

A COSY DRESSING GOWN.

designed for use solely with no foolish leaning towards gratifying the artistic feelings or per- sonal vanity of the wearer. It is so easy nowadays to provide oneself with a pretty gown of this sort, which is very becoming to its owner, and combines all the requirements for comfort, and yet costs not one penny more than its hideous predecessor. This dressing gown was designed for a brunette, and is made of a thick yellow fluffy- surfaced woollen material with a reversible side in which the ground was cream with a zig- zag pattern in black. The yoke is of black chantilly lace over yellow silk, and a broad folded band or scarf of black crepe passes round the foot of the yoke, and is arranged in a bow in front from which two long ends depend, finished with a fine jetted fringe, and em- broidered with butterflies in coloured silks. The loose kimono sleeves have turned back cuffs of black velvet on which gaily-coloured butterflies are also worked. There is nothing in the style of this dressing gown too difficult for a clever girl to undertake herself, as the gown is easy fitting and being thick requires no lining, while the embroidered butterflies

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Princess Margaret and Princess Victoria Patricia of Connaught who are very popular in Society, have been paying aseries of visits since .the Duke mid Duchess of Connaught left for India. They have been staying with Earl and Countess Beauchamp, at Madresfield Court, where Princess Margaret quietly kept her twenty-first birthday on the 15th of this month. Whilst there the Princesses paid a visit to the Royal Porcelain Manu- factory, as Worcester, but Princess Margaret is more fond of music than of art and she is one of the most accomplished pianists in the Royal Family. The two Princesses like Dublin, where they are known, and where when Lady Dudley returns from the Rivierathey will go On a visit to Lord and Lady Dudley at the Viceregal Lodge. The Beauchamp family trace their descent from the time of Edward 1., and Madresfield Court, their ancestral home, is one of the most famous mansions in the country. The founder of the family was Walter de i Beauchamp, who was Steward ,of the Household to I Edward 1., a post filled by Lord Beauchamp's father in the Household of Queen Victoria some years ago. I Madresfield Court played an important part in the Civil War, and it is still surrounded by a moat with a draw I bridge. The gardens are also as famous as the mansion itself. They are enclosed in great avenues of cedars and elms, and each garden has a special class of flower, so that one class or other is in bloom each day of the year.. . Some people are disposed to think that the world is getting too clever. However that may be, another dis- covery is announced in a new direction, viz., that of the 'etherization" of plants, which is intended to force flowers to bloom out of season, and to abridge the natural resting period very considerably. After being twice subjected to the action of ether—on each occa- sion for 48 hours, at a stretch—the plants are removed to a forcing house where it is said, they quickly begin to bloom. Experiments are stated to have been made with blac azaleas.and othersflowers, in most cases with complete success. It is claimed for the new method which has been tried by a Norfolk horticulturist, that it decreases greatly the cost of forcing flowers into bloom during the winter.. If that is so the prices ought 'to come down which is the practical effect that concerns most nearly the majority of people. I At several churches in London the clergy are devis- ing novelties in the hope of attracting more people to ; the services. None however are so entirely successful as the means adopted by Dr. Portens, to secure the regular attendance of his parishioners in the Kentish village of Hinton of which he was rector from 1765 to ; 1787. Dr. Portens was afterwards Bishop • of London, • and his name is well-known in connection with the Act . , of Parliament, with regard to Sunday observance. - Under his will he loft a sum of money to be divided , I annually between six poor cottagers at Hinton who; attended church most regularly. Each recipient lakes ! between .£4 and .£5, and this is not without effect in inducing the villagers to go regularly to church. At one church in London, magic lantern displays prove a great attraction, and at another the Vicar has I recently decided to hold a number of services in the dark. This is opposed to the popular view that many women go to church. to criticise the hats and gowns of other lady members of the congregation, but this par- ticular parish is in a poor district. When the Vicar ; enquired why more women did not attend the church ;he was frequently met with the reply "I cannot afford, Ito dress well enough." He has therefore decidell to hold certain services absolutely in the dark, and, as ; books would be useless, the Psalms and hymns will be thrown on a screen which can be readily seen by worshippers." Among smart women, the demand for white fur pelerines and muffs, has practically ceased, and all the ' best shops have been offering these furs at a really low figure during the sales. They possess many disadvan- tages. To begin with, the rapidity with which they soil even in bright frosty weather, is amazing! Again a mas 3 of white is overpowering and blots out the values of a daintily designed toilette, and yet again to only the quite young, and very bonny face, are these white furs becoming. The modish woman,hosvever, has not given up her pet idea of a note of white about the I throat. A thickly-lined, front and neckband of white chiffon, or crepe de chine, is worn with the fashionable collarless coat, and over this, when the weather de- wands it, a little ermine tie appears. Speaking of the quite jeans fille,there is very welcome simplicity about her entire outfit just now. The ' simplest fabrics and styles only, fall to her share, and absolutely no jewellery, the throat, shoulders, sad arms being left free of ornament, also the hair, which is most simply arranged. For dance dresses, sills tulle is the , ' fabric of the season, then come gauze. silk muslin, net, chiffon, and crêpe de chine. In colours, pink is first favourite, but white is more generally preferred. Smooth cloths will return to favour this coming spring. To the woman who can afford but one 'tailor gown a year, this will be welcome news, as in smooth cloths the suitably cut coatee does as well for indooreis outdoor wear. Regarding colours, quiet tones seem ; likely to reign supreme. At the moment plaids and checks are seen, but the smaller the latter, and more broken the former, the better the effect will be. Shot silks too have come to the front again, blue and green,asad. red and black, being the favourite combinations. Society just now seems to hick both a plaything and a craze. It is tired of ping-pong and of bridge, and seems quite unable at the moment to discover a new fashionable game wherewith to combat .the ennui of a life of leisure. The American Society woman is gener- ally very resourceful in discovering new fads, but then they are usually of a too fantastic nature to be seriously taken up on this side. In some so-called -smart sets euchre parties are all the rage. It is a more gambling game than bridge, but the women are comparatively few of whom cards have an absorbing interest, and it is not an unhealthy sign that so many of them have taken most enthusiastically to the motor car for pleasure and diversion. - How to keep palms and ferns through the winter, is a problem few women—who have neither conservatory or greenhouse properly warmed and ventilated—are able to solve in a successful manner, am' the new idea of real palms and ferns, covered with some sort of invisi- ble composition which makes them everlasting, is likely to he much appreciated. At present these "real " I plants, which require no attention, other that dusting, are somewhat expensive, but they will last, of course, I for an indefinite time, if carefully handled. Those among us, who enjoy on dits will thoroughly ' appreciate the work " Musicians' Wit, Humour, and Anecdote," by Frederick J. Crowest—(The Walter Scott Publishing Co., Limited-3s. :;L)—Tt contains several , hundred stories—the majority :)f them new—of great composers, singers, and inearnmental ists, of all countries and all times. For those int:,reAted in graphology, the book will holden added clueen, as it contains a con- . siderable number of autograptae it is moreover pro-. fusely illustrated with quaint drawings by J. P, Donne. • MAD GE.

EVENTS OF THE wax

THE DEATH OF M. DE BLOWITZ.

M. de Blowitz, the veteran French journalist .who was for many years the Paris correspondent .of the "Times," and who died on Sunday last, was born at Pilsen, in Bohemia, on December 28, 1825, It waa only a few weeks ago that he was pre- sented with a souvenir by his Paris colleagues on his retirement, which took effect at the end of last year. He became a French citizen during the Franco-German war, and joined the staff of the -4‘ Times" a year later. The last time in which he had "the honour to speak in the columns of the Times," was in an article on "France in 1902," which appeared on the last day of the old year. M. de Blowitz was a born journalist— enterprising, tact- fu, shrewd, wielding a ready pen, with which he understoood at once how to in- terest and enter- tain his readers, to serve the best interests of the

public. It was not long before .shis daily telegrams to the "Times," ac- --quired a European importance. Never before had a journalist been so well informed. He was one of the first newspaplr writers to make use of the inter- -view as a means of instructing the public, and there was scarcely a notability of his time who did not yield himself to him. M. 'niers, M. Gambetta, . Prince Bismarck, the late Kin?: of Spain, the :Sultan, and Many other illustrious personages had „recourse to him at times of crisis. A NEW CRZMATORIUM. There can be little doubt that the averse senti- ,m,-,nt awakened when the advocacy of cremation was begun in earnest in this country some years . two is suffering a sure if somewhat slow death. One by one, crematoria are springing up, and now municipal bodies that favour immediate reduction to ashes are the majority. The first municipal crematorium was built in 1901 at Hull, and since then similar institutions have been established in Sheffield, Liverpool, Bournemouth, Exeter, Leices- ter, and Glasgow. At these, cremations are of ;almost daily occurrence. A new crematorium has been erected for London at Golder's Hill, Hendon. Previously the nearest crematorium to the metro- Tons has been that at Woking—twenty-four miles .distant—but Golder's Hill, Hendon, is within four ,or five miles of the Marble Arch. C. B. FRY'S DEBUT.

The announcement that C. B. Fry would make his debut for Portsmouth against Millwall at Fratton rark in a Western League match on Wednesday, greatly interested the football world of the South. and many were those individual resolhtions the result in a big gate. The Ports- mouth Club are naturally anxious that the famous amateur should obtain as much practice with hi i new colleagues as possible prior to the Cup tie at Everton, and with this object in view they desired to have him transferred from Southampton for Southern League matches, so that he might play for Ports- mouth against Bristol lovers this week. Mr. F r y himself agreed to this suggestion, and it was not ex- pected that the "Saints" would offer any objec- tion to the pro- posal. The feel- ing in Ports- mouth is that the club have acted wisely in accepting th e ft ports manlike offer of the old Oxonian. The report wam circu- c. D. FRY lated last week ,WHO NOW PLATS NCR PORTS- MOUTH.

that Mr. Fry had applied to the Football Association for his transfer to Ports- mouth in the Southern League. As a matter of fact, Mr. Fry actually registered himself as a playing member for Portsmouth on February 9, so that there can be no question about his being eligible for the Cup tie. He merely signed a Southern League form, in order that he might obtain more practice with Portsmouth. Mr. Fry is one of the best educated footballers we have. He has taken the Oxford degree of 8.A., after being educated at Repton and Wadham College, Oxford.

TWO IMPORTANT NAVAL ADDITIONS.

Towards the close of last week an interesting -ceremony took place at Portsmouth, when the cruiser Snitio/k, which belongs to what is known as the county class, was launched by Lady Stradbroke in the presence of an enormous crowd of spectators, who braved the intensely cold weather. The launch was effected without a hitch, and SO well was everything arranged that by the mere severing of a rope the new cruiser was auto- matiettlly released and glided smoothly into the leptFr. The Suffolk, designed by Sir William hite, has for some while been a familiar sight to those pa.sing up and down POtsmouth Harbour. i She s an armoured cruiser of 9,800 tons displace- ment, and, although she will carry no heavier guns than 6-inch quick-firers, she promises to be a very doughy opponent to far larger vessels on account ,of her numerous armaments and armoured protect- ions, which are tour and five inches thick. She -possesses an armoured deck, armoured bulkheads, ,ten armoured casements and two armoured turrets : 'and 'is indeed a ship of war. The length of the :Suffolk is 440 ft., with a beam of 66 ft..,. and i with twin screws she s expected to do 23 knots. She appears a little peculiar at first sight on account of stumpy topmasts; but she belongs to ,a highly serviceable class, and in time of war should prove an extraordinarily useful 'vessel. The following day there was laid down upon .the slip, from which the Suffolk was launched, the battleship New Zealand, which belongs to the King Edward VII class, and is of 16,500 Una displacement, and will be upon her ..com- -pletion one of the most powerful battleships in the world. Of 18,000 horse-power, she will be up to 18 knots, and will carry 2000, tons of coal. ,Zhe is IQ ft. in length, .78 it, in boadth, with

a draught of just on 27 ft., and she will— although cairying no fighting-tops, each mast carrying nothing but a searchlight-top—be very heavily armed with big gnus and strongly

THE COMMAND OF THE FOURTH ARMY CORPS.

The latest Army Gazette was a very interesting and important one, as it contained the official announcemPnt of several leading appointments in the .service. Chief of these, and one of the most unexpected, was that of Lieutenant-General Lord Grenfell, G.C.8., G.C.M.G., to command the Fourth Army Corps, that is, the East Anglian one with its headquarters at Colchester. This distinguished officer, who i, about to vacate the Governorship of Malta, was, according to constant rumour, intending to hang up his arms after his long service, and, therefore, his selection for the still to be formed Fourth Army Corps came as a pleasant surprise to his friends. THE STANLEY AUTOMOBILE SHOW.

The first of the many motor car exhibitions to be held between now and the early summer is the Stanley Automobile Show, at Earl's Court, which opened its doors last week under the au,pices of the Stanley Cycling Club, who hold vn annual show of cycles at the Agricultural Hall every winter. Their present exhibition is not so much large as interesting, and it contains plenty of things to fascinate the automobilist, especially if he be of a patriotic turn of mind, as British cars are particularly well represented. Of all the finest stalls is that of the Lanchester Company, of Birmingham, who show no less than elevon txamp.es of their much admired car. SURGZRY WITHOUT THE Wilt- E

Dr. Lorenz, a remarkably clever Austrian specialist, has discovered that many surgical operations which have hitherto necessitated the use of the knife, can be trewed more successfully and with less pain by a bloodless plastic method. Dr. Lorenz has lately been giving demonstratious to London surgeons who were greatly impressed at the success of this treatment. Wnen Dr. Lorenz visited Ameria to cure little Lolita Armour of congenital dislocation of the hip, the medical facia!), marvelled at his system of treatment ; but they did not understand how far-reaching it was, and what miracles might be accompli-hed by it. Dr. Lorenz has since then travelled across the entire American continent and back again, and has performed his operations free in a dozen cities. He has cured thousands of • crippled children, writ's() case i were hitherto regarded as hopeless. He has made them straight and whore, able to Walk and to do their life's work. It is no exoggera- don to appty the term " miracles of Acience " to Dr. Lorenz's opfrations, for he has cured cases which were re- garded as hope- less, even with the most radical use of the knife. DR. LORENZ Congenital dis- TEM: INV F.NTOR OF PLASTIC location of the. :SURGERY. hip is only one

of the many de• fortuities of chil- dren to which his Tstem is applicable. Among the other common deformities which Dr. Lorenz has cured by bloodless surgery are wry neck, curvature of the spine, club f(et, knot:k knees. and bow legs. The greater number of chil- dren's deformities are due to some displacement of the muscles or soft parts, and not to malfortirition of the bones. To all deformities of this character bloodless surgery is applicable. For instance, club foot is due to the twisting up of the foot in a spiral form,-the forward part of the foot usually being turned very much in. Dr. Lorenz seizes this spiral with his powerful hands, and unrolls it till it assumes the natural shape of a foot. The common method is to cut into bones muscles and tendonft until the foot is as nearly riatugal as possible..

The Examiner, Friday, January 23, 1903.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

St. Paul's Church.

A large congregation assembled in this church on Sunday evening when the choir rendered Maunder's sacred cantata "Penitence, Pardon and Peace." The singing throughout was admirable, and gave evidence of carefpl training. Miss Armstrong and Mr. Gelderd were the soloists and acquitted themselves with great credit. Mr. E. Wilkes presided at the organ with his usual skill.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church.

On Sunday, the Rev. J. Gardham Reed occupied the pulpit in the morning, and also led the adult Bible Class, of which thereare over 70 members, in the after- noon. Mr. Albert Calvery from Widnes, preached at night, when the Lord's Prayer set to music was sung, also a hymn tune composed by the organist, Mr. Fred Podmore, both being heartily appreciated.

A SCHOOLBOY'S SAD DEATH. Fatal Fianellette Again.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, conducted an inquiry at the Police Station on Monday afternoon relative to the death of Albert Proctor Jones, aged 13 years and 11 months, only son of Mr. James Proctor Jones, of Sunny- side, Latchford.—The only witness called was the father of the deceased boy, who informed the Court that he was an estate agent, and lived at 661, Knutsford-road. At eight o'clock on Thursday morning last week his son went downstairs to attend to the postman at the front door. There was a penny to pay on one of the letters, and the lad came upstairs for it. Witness gave it to him, and the boy went downstairs with it. Shortly afterwards witness heard screams and ran downstairs. He found the deceased at the bottom of the stairs with his flannelette shirt on fire. Ile obtained a rug,wrapped it round him, and extinguished the flames. Doctors Leadbetter and Bowden were called in and attended the boy until he died on Friday evening. Previous to his death, witness's son told him that after,he had given thq postman the penny he went to the kitchen fire and knelt on the fender to warm himself when his shirt caught _ _ _

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and passed a vote of sympathy with Mr. Jones and his _

—The' Coroner, in canveying the vote of sympathy, also expressed his -sorrow at the sad °cc:armee. It may be added that th 3 boy should have taken a part in the con- cert given by the scholars attending People's College in aid of local charities, on the evening of the unfortun- ate accident. The news of his death came as a great shock to the scholars and friends of the deceased boy.— The grave closed over his remains on Tuesday at the Cemetery. Many wreaths were forwarded from sym- pathising friends and fellow-scholars.

ONE EVIL LEADS TO ANOTHER. --- - A Miner's Strange Experience.

When a child builds up with wooden brrelis a tine house, and then removes the bottom brick, his little eyes open wide with surprise when the whole pile collapses. The human body is built up of so many living systems each dependent upon another like the bricks in the child's house. Yet many people wonder how it is that theyhave so many ailments. They have so many because they have one! In other words some important function becomes deranged and throws all else out of gear. The digestion goes wrong say : this disorders the blood, spoils the appetite, robs the body of its energy, and reduces its tone. Thusaffected, prevalent diseases sometimes creep in and loail because one brick is pulled out as it were. Mr. Wm. Buxton, a miner of Ebenezer-street, Awsworth, Ilkeston, says .—" Some years ago I began to suffer very badly from indigestion. I could not eat a proper meal, and the pains after even scanty meals were terrible. At times they were so great, that I would have to move about the house in a doubled-up position, catching ho Id c tb' f 'litre to help me along.

To the pains of indigestion were next added the horrors of biliousness. The sickness and retching nutde me so bad that I often thought T should never got well again. Probably because my system got run down completely I then .contraeted intluetwa. My doctor attended me, but I received very little good from his treatment. Then I read about Chas. Forde's Bile Beans, and 1 determined to give them a trial. Soon after commencing with the first box I felt au improvement. I seemed to grow stronger, my appetite began to • return, and the indigestion Antoci. I per- severed with the Beans, and the biliousness also. disap- peared. I.am now quite cured of all my old-time ail- ments, and able to work my shift regularly and ener- getically." Here is powerful proof of what cures- Chaff. Forde's Bile Beans can effect. Wherever the liver is dis Ordered, the digestiottwrong, the blood impure and the vitality ; low, then are Bile Beans a boon. St. Peter's Church.

The Rev. M. W. Larcombe, M.A., vicar of St. Aidan's Liverpool preached the annual sermons on behalf of the Church Pastoral Aid Societyat this church on Sunday. In the afternoon the rev, gentleman spoke at the men's service on "Gambling." said that several speakers had defined gambling as stealing, but he did not look upon it in that light, but as a transference of property from one to another on an element of chance. We were stewards of the money that was entrusted to us, or which we had earned, and we had no right to dispose of it by gambling. Miss Alice Bridge was the soloist, and there was a good attendance. Mr, P. Edgar was at the organ morning and evening, and Mr. F. Webster, assist- ant organist, presided at the instrument at the after- noon service.

===== 0002601_19030123_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
Good Templar S ermons at Warrington.

On Sunday last, special services were held in Ebenezer'Church, under the auspices of the 1.Q.G.T., Temperance Mission Lodge. There VCELS a good congre- gation at the morning service when the Rev. J. A. Lee, D.C.T. (Liverpool), was the preacher. He based his sermon on the words, "But, beloved, be not ignorant of one thin,,, that one day is with the Lord as a thousand years, and a thousand years as one day," 2 Peter, 111. A man's li fe,he said,was not measured by the number of years he had lived, but by the amount of good work he had accomplished. In point of years, the oldest man that ever lived was Methuselah, but what have we in remembrance of him? Absolutely nothing but the dates of his birth and death. In his opinion, the oldest man was the man who was only thirty-three years on this earth and was then crucified for the sins of the world. He urged all present not to let a single day pass without something done for God. In the afternoon, a children's musical service was held, when Mr. F. Merlin presided. Four girls from the Juvenile Temple sang "Come, sign the pledge," Mr. J. Sheath recited Long- fellow's "Psalm of Life." and a duet, "Charity," was rendered by Misses E. Lee and K. Bowyer. Rev. J. A. Lee gave an address to the children on kindness, taking for his text "Words fitly spoken are as apples of gold in baskets of silver."—Prov. xxvi. 11.—Mrs. Lee, D.v.T., had charge of the evening service, when the place was full. She gave a very interesting discourse on Jesus Christ as the burden-bearer, speaking from the words "My people have gone into captivity because they have no knowledge."—lsaiah v. 13. Special hymns were sung during the thy, and at the evening service, instead of the usual anthem, the choir effectively rendered "Look not upon the wine." Mr. J. H. Thornton presided at the organ. The members of the lodge sat in regalia at all the services and presented a pleasing appearance.

LANCASHIRE LEAGUE

EARLESTOWN V. • STALYBRIDGE ROVERS.—Played at Earlestown, before about 500 spectators. The recent severe weather had rendered the ground very hard and slippery and the players found it very difficult to keep their feet. The home skipper Won the toss, and decided to play against the hill, Davies setting the ball in motion on behalf of the Rovers. The Earlestown team included two new men in Swift,a custodian from Bolton and Cox, a left outside from Preston. Play was chiefly fought out in the home half, and Whittaker had very little to do. A long shot from. BArtley provided the custodian with his solitary piece of work for some time. A faulty clearance by CoHinson enabled Limond to drop the leather right in the goal Mouth, but both Bartley and cox allowed a golden opportunity to pass, and the danger was easily averted. Interval : No score. Final S!alybridge 2, Earlestown 0.

EXHAUSTED NERVES.

"T suffered for years from nervous exhaustion andit made my life a misery." This is how Mr. Alex. Dan- can, of Cothill Alves, by Forres, N. 8., began his letter on May 18th, 1902. Mr. Duncan is not the only one who has had to suffer from the daily torture of nerves,which leaves the brain exhausted, unlit for its daily duty, and racks the body with pain. Here are some of Mr. Duncan's symptoms. Sufferers will recognise them at'once. " I had pains in the small of ink back and also in my head, always felt tired, and had trembling in my limhs. I slept badly and had horrible dreams, and got up as tired as when I went to bed. My appetite was poor, and I was reduced to skeleton. I became very despondent, and I was afraid 'was never going to get back my health and strength." But there was no need for despondency. There is a cure for.everyone, and Mr. Duncan found it in Dodd's Kidney Pills. THE NORTHERN UNION. THE LEAGUE.---FIRST DIVISION.

BRIGHOUSE RANGERS v. SWINTON. A liberal coating of straw and the gentle influence of a steam roller, had rendered the Brighouse ground at Lane Head quite fit for play, and there was a good attendance to see how the local side would perform against the clever Swinton team. A bad error by Oakes, who kicked into the centre of the field instead of , touching down, gave a fine opportunity to Evans, who opened the scoring by kicking a goal. *Then a fine round of passing among the Swinton backs put Valentine in I possession, and clearing the opposition, he paved the way for a try by Lewis, Evans failing at goal. Brig- house were kept on the defence, and Williamson getting I hold from ascrimmage, tricked Bevan and ran over with the second try.. From Evans' kick the ball hit the post, and bounced back into the field of play. At half-time Swinton led by eight points to nil. Lewis Brooke was hurt and had to retire just before the interval, but in spite of his absence Brighouse kept their Iline intact for a long time after the change of ends, the three-quarters doing some effective tackling. Twice Hampson was almost in at the left corner after very fine sprints. At length Valentine • shook off the opposition and got over, and later,the Swinton captain scored again after clever fielding, Chorley landing a goal from the second try. The result was an easy victory for Swinton by 2 goals 4 tries (16 points), to Brighouse, nil. BROUGHTuN RANGERS v. OLDHAM Despite the cold weather there were 10,000 people at. Wheater's Field, Broughton, to witness the above match. Oram started for the Rangers, and Bent failing to re- turn, scrummaging took place in the visitors' territory. Play was eventually taken to the centre, following which, Cash from touch near the centre, put in a grand screw kick into touch in gaol in the Rangers' half. Here several scrummages were formed, and the ball ~being passed out by Sam Lees (jun.) to Tyson, the last- named dropped a goal. Following tricky work by Sam James, Wilson missed a long pass and a good oppor- tunity of scoring for the Rangers. Soon afterwards Oldham attacked, and from a lengthy throw by Brady, Spottiswoode gathered and ran in a fine try. S. Lees , (jun.) failed from the place. Score :—Oldham 1 goal ; and 1 try (5 points), Broughton Rangern nil. HULL v. HULL KINGSTQN ROVERS. Fully 10,000 spectators crowded the Craven-street ; enclosure to witness this battle between the rival clubs, ! and naturally, keen interest mingled with excitement was the predominating feeling among lovers of the ; game. Fortunately the ground, which had not been Icovered during the frost, was in first-rate condition. The result was--lull Kingston Rovers, 2 goals, 2 tries (10 points); Hull nil. BRADFORD v. BATLEY. Played at Park'Avenue. The ground had been well protected, and was in a fair condition, Bradford being rewarded by an attendance of some 8,009 spectators. , Winning the toss, Bradford took advantage of a slight , wind, and played away from the Park. If anything.the3,- had a slight advantage in the first half, though the for- ward play was poor. Ward, nearing h.:a-time, dropped a very smart goal. The teams crossed over with the score unaltered, and the opening stages were again in I favour ef Bradford. Thanks to a smart kick and follow up by Dunbavin, Heseltine was afforded a chance of picking up and running in a try, of which he promptly aVailed himself. He failed, however, at goal. From this point the Batley forwards took the command, Brad- ford being continuously pressed. With the game beneath the home posts, a smart movement amongst the visiting backs sent the ball out to Auty, who, having been left unwatched, was able to run in an easy try, from which Davies kicked a goal. Bradford now fell away, and towards the close Hodgson, by dropping a goal, won the match for Batley. Result :—Batley, 2 goals one try (7 ; Bradford, 1 gaal I try (5 points). ,

The Wesley Guild.---A United Rally. sunday and Monday, the members of the Bold- street Wesley Guild held their annual festival, and. additionid in crest was lent to the event by the visit of the Rev. W. B. Fitz-Gerald, the Connexional Guild secretary. The services on Sunday were a great success and on Monday evening a" united rally " was held. To this meeting, Miss Prince had issued invi- tations on behalf of the Bold-street Guild to neig;ic bwring branches of the Guild and aho lo several- Chrit,tian Endeavour societies. Representatives were present from Bewsey-road, Padgate, Latchford, Lymm, Halton, and Widnes Guilds. the Ragged School Christian Endeavour, and the Working lien's Mission (Bank Quay) Christian Endeavour. Mr J. G. Withmhaw, of renketh, i!resided, and WAR supported by the Circuit ministerg and the Rev. W. B. Fitz-(erald (the Connexional GO) secretary), and 11r. Littler and Miss Prin.- The Chairman said there was no doubt the Wesley ()Oct outcemG Of a movement in Methodism to reCOAll;se tilt: young people and to induce them to work for the Church. lie remPapologies for absence frf in the Legh-strcet scciety and from the Rev. J. F. Matthews (Latchford ( liristian Endeavour). Miss Prince read the report of the 13°1d-street Guild, which shovted that there was a total of .78 inembers, com- prising 40 active members, 28 companion members, and JO associate members. There was a balance due to the treasurer f 2s. 2(1., which was accounted. for by the necessary outlay in connection with their open-air meetings. That amount was only small compared with tLe amount of stock in possession of the guild, but they hoped to clear the debt off at that meeting. (Applause.) Interesting addresses on g,uild. work were delivered by lin Littler and the Rev. W. B. Fitz-Gerald.--The Rev. G. H. Carnson called the roll of the local branches which was responded to.— The meeting was also briefly addressed by the Rev. R. Wilkins Rees.---An ackno.wledgment of the Chan.. man's and the Rev. W. B. Fitz-Gerald's services wag made on the proposition of the Rev. W. C. J. Neville, seconded_by Mr. Sherburne.

The manager of the Casino Theatre, Brest, having announced that, despite the authoritiet,, he will stage ii piece founded on the Humbert affair, the officer com- mandinp• the garrison there has been instructed to sur- round the theatre with troops, so that no one will be able to enter.

A double execution took place at Dawson City, Klondike, on Tuesday. The men were named Fournier and Label!, and were convicted of a triple murder on the Steward River last June.

UNDER A TnArrioN ENGINE.—Walter Cunningham, 36, Kelsall, near Chester, was walking in advance of a traction engine near Delamere Station, on Wednesday, when by some unexplained means he was knocked down. The traction engine wheels passed over him, mangling his body and killing him almost immediately. TOBEIt MUNI' CENTENARIAN DEAD.—The death has occurred at the Baptist Manse, Tobermory, of Miss Mary M`Rinnon, at the advanced age of 102. She was born in the parish of Mull, in which she died. Until recently her faculties remained clear and unclouded, and she could recount with much vividness the many changes she had seen in the Highlands during her long life. She often spoke of the time when her brother was captured in the press gang days. That happened in 1815.

PAPER RULED TO ANY PATTERN BEWSEY STREET WARRINGTON.

THURSDAY was the second THE KING AND ahniversary of the day on THE COUNTRY. which King EDWARD VII.

succeeded to the throne. The longest reign in our history came to a quiet close on January 22nd, 1901. We were in the midst •of a war which had occasioned the very gravest disquietude to the aged Queen VICTORIA. "Give peace in our time, 0 Lord," is a supplication that was never offered with more sincerity than by the great and kindly SOVEREIGN who passed away two years ago. The greatness of disposition and the largeness of heart which beheld war as an event to be detested and mourned over, whatever might be its cause, and however just its occasion, were inherited, as was soon to appear, by Queen VICTORIA'S successor. Englishmen always view with jealousy any interference of the Crown with Ministerial functions and duties; but there was a throb of satisfaction throughout the nation at large,when it was rumoured that the KING was personally endeavouring to forward the cause of peacc4 ; and at length, when th 3 war actually came to a conclusion, there was everywhere a delighted conviction that it was the SOVEREIGN, and not the Ministers, who had suggested the terms on which it might be possible to bring the long- drawn-out conflict to an end.

King EDWARD was unusually happy in the cir- cumstances of his accession, for he succeeded to the affections of the people. We recall those days when gib late Mr. BRADLAUGH was lecturing throughout the country on the House of Han- over. There was then, a serious possibility of an anti-monarchial movement. There was an actual Republican party in the country, not merely a stray Republican here and there. Why, even Mr. CHAMBERLAIN was a Republican, and in that char- acter he had endeavoured to enter Parliament as member for Sheffield. There were men in Parlia- ment who described their position in a softened form of language as that of "Republicans in theory." Sir CHARLES DILKE stirred the whole country by his Newcastle speech on the costliness of monarchy. The temper of the public mind has
