-wholly changed since then. We do not now rake up the history of the House of Hanover. We do

not work out sums bearing upon the cost of the 'Crown. The nation is contentedly loyal; and it is to be noted in especial that there has been no per- ceptible change in the attitude of the people towards the throne in the two years that have ,passed since the change in the occupants of that lofty seat. What the late Queen was to her sub- jects Edward VII. has become to his. There is 'nowhere any whisper of disaffection. On the con. trary, there has been an extraordinary display of national loyalty and affection in connection With an illness which, but for exceptional medical and surgical skill, might again have left the throne vacant before the new KING had been 'crowned in the ancient and kingly way. EDWARD VII. succeeded to his place in the world's hierarchy when he had become ripe in yearf; and experience. His character and his qualities were 'known. Tact, a fine and noble courtesy, a quiet dignity of manner, an Imostentatious consciousness of the high place he fills, an abo•inding kindliness of nature, and a constant considerateness for all classes of his subjects, are the distinctive traits of King EDWARD VII. The people recognise and rejoice at this choice mingling of admirable 'characteristics. The Sovereignty is not felt as a weight in this country, but cheerfully sustained as an object of pride and national gratification. , _

" No, sir," says Mr. DOOLEY in his latest book; "I don't want to be a ICING, an' whiniver I see a good fellow takin' th' job I feel sorry f'r him." There is more than a jest in the observation. It may not be too frequently true in these days that "uneasy lies the head thai. wears a crown" ; but the price that has to be paid personally by the SOVEREIGN for the privilege of exercising Sovereignty is, even in a quiet and loyal country like our own, very considerable. The burden of public affairs is left at Windsor or at Buckingham Palace as well as in Downing-street. The ICirto, who likes pleasure, never skirks work. The assi- duity which distinguished him when he was PRINCE OF WALES, and which enabled him to re- lieve the late QUEEN on so many occasions of functions which would have been unduly harden_ some .to her sex and her years, :has become still more a characteristic since he came tu the throne. He has obviously determined to be no roi faineant. He has exhibited a constant and keen interest in events and in the welfare of his people. He is not merely the chief of a society, but a leader in social movements. So far as we know him, indeed .he is the ideal head of a State, and on the second anniversary of his accession we shall only be ex_ pressing a general sentiment when we say, in time-honoured words," Long may he reign !"

THE future of South THE SOUTH AFRI- Africa depends almost CAN LABOUR wholly on some reason- PROBLEM. able settlement of the labour. The Rand million- . Aires believed that the labour question would be 4settled by the war. They maintained that the Boer Government stood between them and the opportunity of getting more work out of the black man for a smaller payment in money. It has been shown in printed documents, and in speeches made to company meetings in the City 'al London, that the goldmines would yield an xtett £2,000,000 of profit annually as soon,as the native labourer could be more stringently dealt With. Ti‘is view of the facts has b4en held no to ,ridicule by the course of events. The Rand capi- talists proceeded to reduce the price of black labolr in the mines immediately after • the war. The customary rate of payment had been £3 to £3 10s. per month. A monthly wage of 30s. was agreed upon as sufficient for -the mines to pay for Kaffir labour.. The mine- owners assumed that they could fix wages without the assent of the workmen. This wculd seem ae 'odd conclusion for men of busineseto arrive at ; but the truth is that these wealthy men were not troubling themselves with economic considera- tions; they were looking for the Government to do something to drive the Kaffir into the mines. If h's huts could be taxed in such a wty that he would be made to feel the burden, if his wives .could all be taxed, the tax growing heavier as -they became more numerous, then something might be done for European views of morality and for the owners of the mines at the same time. •Plurality of wives might become less cornmon,anti the Kaffir would go to the mines for the purpose 'of at least earning as much money as would pay ibis taxation. But notwithstanding its faults and failings in other directions, the Government has :not as yet 'consented to become a recruiting agency for pro- vidiog black labour for the Rand millionaires. Even. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has gone no turther than the declaration that unless the black race is settled dii regular industry it will become a difficulty. Under these circumstances it is found necessary 'to abandon the idea that another couple of millions ,a year could be drawn out of the mines by making the Kaffir work bard for the barest means of sub- .aistence. The keeping down of wages to the level of 30s. a month has been abandoned as an impracticable idea, and the rates of pay- ment have now gone up to the old level, without, however, producing the expected result, and bringing the black labourers , back to the mines. About half as many Kaffir labourers are now working in the Rand district as were at work there before the war. And it seems ;for the present as if no more were to be obtained %There are 10,000,000 blacks in our South African 'Colonies, but those who can be induced to work for wages only amount to 5,000 or so. The supply is Wholly inadequate to the needs of the case, and hence the mine-owners are turning their eyes else- where,first to Uganda,and then to the teeming and overflowing millions of China. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has' performed an essential service in South Africa 'first by discouraging the idea that the people at home will look with Satisfaction on a large itn.: Portation of Chinamen into South Africa, and then by reminding the Rand millionaires that a people which has made such sacrifices as we have done for the purpose of extinguishing slavery is not likely to tolerate the establishment of slavery in another form because the owners of South African goldmines will not pay the white man a sufficient wage and cannot induce the black man to labour underground. No large step will be made towards the settlement of the South African

labour question until the Rand capitalists are • clearly given_to understand that they must base their expectations of profit-earning on the usual 2ornmercia 1 grounds, and that the Imperial Government doee not exist for the pirpost, of increasing their dividends by applying compulsion to their workmen, black or white or yellow. Row is it that we hear of half-a-crown a" day a

the highest possible wages for other than skilled labour on the Rand? Half-a-crown a day may be

taken as the lowest wage paid for labour in our own country, where the means of subsistence are almost immeasurably less costly. Much more than that would seem to be the natural wage of even the meanest labourer on a rich goldfield like the Rand. Whilst the labour question is to the front, however, it is alleged that the Rand is not a rich goldfield. The Transvaal mines are low- grade mines, we are assured. Their working cost must be kept at a low rate. Many of then must be closed if it becomes necessary to replace black labour by white, or if yellow labour is to be kept out, or if some compulsion cannot be used towards the blacks. This would be all true; but it -would seem more credible if the great houses in Park-lane were not passing out of the hands of the English aristocracy into those of the Rand millionaires. Reducing profits does not seem to hive been thought of as one of the pos- sible modes of dealing with the labour question. It is worth consider*, however. If South Africa is to become a possession on which we can pride ourselves as an important and prosperous portion , of a great Empire, the success of its industries must not depend on the oppression of the black, but on the continually increasing utilisation of the white man.

We hope our Conservative friends will bear in mind the criticisms of Mr. Winston Churchill, M.P., on the Government's scheme of Army Re- form. They are apt to sneer at and discount the criticism of political opponents, who seldom, how- ever, hit so hard and so savagely as has the junior Tory member for Oldham. Bo pummelled Mr. Brodrick's pot theories into an even more shape- less mass than they were originally and if the supporters of the Government can stand home truths and cold facts when presented by one of themselves they should profit by Mr. Churchills' tirade to an appreciable extent. "After two years , have gone by "says Mr. Churchill, "I have no hesitation in saying that Mr. Brodrick's scheme of I 1901 is a total, costly, ghastly, failure." This will not make pleasant reading for our honeymooning War Minister ! Then the merciless critic went on to talk about our skeleton army and phantom reserve and--" what of this wonderful army corps scheme which was going to make England a military nation; which was going to make Mr. Brodrick's reputation equal to if not greater than that of Louvois or Cardwell i> The first Army Corps was not yet half formed; the Second Army Corps was a more skeleton ; the Third Army Corps was incomplete; the Fourth Army Corps con- sisted of Lord Grenfell ; the Fifth, he understood, was in a pigeon-hole at the War Office; and as for the sixth, he believed Mr. Brodrick had taken it with him in a red box on his cruise in the Mediterranean." Finally, Mr. Churchill gave his hearers the benefit of his own ideas as to the mili- tary necessities of the nation. "We do not want in England a kind of imitation German army on a small scale," was the sum total of his criticism and the basis of his suggestions was " a professional army to garrison the Empire, a volunteer citizen army to defend the British Islands, and above all a great, powerful, and splendid navy,strong enough to preserve upon a solid foundation the peace of the civilised world." The speech was punctuated by the cheers of an audience which appeared to delight in the speaker's sharp thrusts, but which, eventually, with true Tory Consistency and obedience to the leading string.passed a "vote of confidence in the Government."

It is refreshing to hear that the Stockton Heath Parish Council are at last in agreement with the Corporation with respect to the proposed exten- sion of the tramway system to Stookton Heath The Corporation have granted them all the con- cessions they have asked for,so that the" Heathens" would in.leed be an ungracious lot if they were not satisfied now. There is, however,a still more serious obstacle to be overcome, ere the Corporation can extend the tramway system to the square at Stock- ton Heath. Tne Cheshire County Council is from some cause or other showing great solicitude for the residents' safety and has petitioned the Board of Trade to require the Corporation to widen Oki London-road beyond the limits agreed to with the Parish Council, and also to undertake other obliga- tions with regard to lighting and other matters. The Board of Trade has decided to hold an inquiry into the subject in London at an early date. The Town Clerk, in his letter to the Parish Council, expresses the opinion that the Corporation are being put to unnecessary expense in having an inquiry forced upon them. Mr. Whittle does not stand alone in this opinion, and it is not surpris- rig to hear that the Corporation, have decided ip the event of the conditions asked for, bY the County Connell being imposed by the Board of Trade, not to proceed with the order.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORRESPONDENT.]

Up to Tuesday this week King Frost ruled .with an iron sway. The weather was bitterly. cold and the majority anticipated there would be a 'heavy fall of snow. Happily for the poorer classes these predictions were falsified. The weather has 'againarrayed.itself in a genial spring-like garb to the satisfaction of the workers. For the greater portion of last week work was at a standstill, the "down locks men," the painters, stone workers etc., being obliged to suspend work on account of the . frost. The tradesfolk are universally grumbling at the state of the town, and,, in this respect, yre being kept well employed for want of something better to do. The lake at the common_ was coated with ice early last week and on Friday and Satur- day was strong enough to bear with the result that there were crowds of men, women and children eager to taste the first fruits of winter. Luckily there were no real mishaps, though. I believe, otta or two were unfor- tunate 'enough to get Immersed to the middle. Atithe time of writing it appears as though the frost were disappearing very nicely indeed.

The now licensing Act has affected Runcorn some- what considefably. Since the New Year there has practically been a d-arth of the usual " drunk and disorderly" at the Police Court. I heard the other day of a well-known local character moaning out the heart rending complaint : "No one will serve me now and I can't get anybody to fetch me a pint." This, itself, is sufficient to explode the idea that men cannot be made tiiipenite by Acts of Parliament.

The Library Committee have wisely decided to stop, if possible, the petty annoyance in the reading room caused by young urchins who tear up magazines, walk noisily about the room, and play well-knowlrinfantile pranks to the annoyance of the readers. The next time one of these juvenile offenders is caught his name and address will be taken, and in the fulness of time his parents: will be advised of his unseemly behaviour. This will, no doubt, prove sufficient to deter such mischiev- ous youngsters from repeating the offences.

There have been no more actual outbreaks of' the small-pox epidemic in Runcorn though an additional case has been reported to the medical officer. As a matter of fact the case is an imported one and is not the result of the previous outbreak in the town.

ALLEGED SWINDLE AT RUNCORN.

At Runcorn on Thursday Joseph Spencer, alias Harry Randall, a middle-aged man, was charged with stealing a watch and chain belonging to Christopher Welling. Superintendent Ennion said the prisoner came to Run- corn on the 11th inst., and obtained lodgings at Mrs. Gridiths's, 25, Parker-street, bystating he was employed on the Ship Canal. On Mo;tday morning he departed without setiiling his bill, and afterwards it was dis- covered that he had taken Wellings's (also a lodger) watch and chain. He was apprehended at Nantwich on Wednesday with the watch and,chain in his possession. He wak remanded for a week, so that his career may be investigated.

NEWTON-IN-iVIAKERFIELD LECTURES COMMITTEE. alHnge on "Ants."

tinder the auspices of the above Committee, the first of the course of Gilchrist lectures was given in the Town Hall, Earlestown. on Monday evening. The Rev. W. H, Daflinger, D.D., D.C.L., was the lecturer, and his subject was " Ants : a Study of Sociology and Politics amongst Insects." _

Coronel R. Pilkington, M.P., presided, and there was a very large audience. The Chairman did not think that Dr. Dellinger required any introduction from him ; he was so very well known, and the reputation of his lectures was so well iknoWn that any remarks of his would be simply wasting their time. Dr. Dellinger said the incomprehensible vastness of this universe and the stupendous improve- ments and energy which were being disclosed, in which science is.enforcing itself t:more and more

upon the minds of our race, nitM became" More impressed with the minute details which open themselves to human research. The ants because they were little feeble folk but roughly endowed for their work in nature,demanded

our sindi. They were mentally. Peers amongst invertebrm animals. it was admitted that though so small they were at the apex of invertebros animals. Ants Were, socially, animals with remarkable and varied civilisations. They provided doors, galleries; nurseries and store rooms,they understand and perform the busi- ness of their life, they collect and preserve food for the dark end unproductive days, they keep the equivalent of mulch cows and other domestic animals; tkey lay seiges, (wry on warfare, do battle, take prisoners and actually make slaves by millions, while they care for,nurture and educate their children with increasing care. They practically converse, they are sometimes pampered by indolence into a helpless aristocracy and show a strong capacity for drunkenness. Could the analogy to humm nature be more complete than that?: The ants were strictly insects and their distribution Was exceedingly wide. The inhabitants of an ant colony consisted of

hreo grades Of individuals, the male's, females and Letters.

From this point the lecture was delivered in darkness,. owing to illustrations by the oxy-hydrogen lantern,and the greatest interest was manifested in the descriptions of the structure (anatomically) of the ant, its brain, tongue, eyes, &c. The other illustrations were descrip- tive of the architectural abilities of the ants, their philanthropy and kindness, their recreation, &c. The Rev. G. Cook, at the Close moved a vote of thanks to the lecturer, which the Rev. J. Carson sesonled, and it was tunnimously accorded. Dr. Dallinger briefly res- ponded, and moved a vote of thanks to the Chairman which wos accorded heartily.

ST. PAUL'S CHURCH TEA PARTY. Enjoyable Gathering at Parr Hail.

The principal and perhaps, most enjoyable social function of the year in connection with St. Paul's Church is the annual parochial tea party and entertain- ment. On Monday evening Parr Hall was the sce.ae of this pleasant gathering which was attended with g rat i fy- ng.success. About 400 parishioners and friends sat down to an excellent tea, the following ladies presiding at the tables :—Mesdames Bracecamp, J. H. Smethurst, Chorley, Smethurst, Burgess, • George Smith, Robinson, Binks, Richardson, Lindley, Locker, Chadwick, Wainwright,Collin, Cb.antry, Bunch, 'Jolley, Reid, Hankinson, Cox, Healey,Partridge.Lawton,Wo rral Lea rt is, Gre Rough, and Powell, Misses Fox, .Geddes, Finney, Pierpaint, Bainbridge, Taylor, A.shurst, Seward. itimmer, and Greenough. . . . . .

Prior 6 the entertainment several speeches with re- gard to the work of the parish were delivered.. Alder- man Smethurst presided in the unavoidable absence of , Alderman Sutton, and at the outset wished all preseat a happy New Year. Referring to the work of the past year he said he was pleased they had a vicar who was so zealous in his work as the ,IRev.W. Bracecamp. The speaker alluded to the increase in the congregations and said he thought that within his recollectioa St. Paul's Church had never been as well attended,as it was at the present time. During the ' year there had also been a marked increase in the amounts of the collections, arid it was gratifying to note that a week ago they raised £l5 for the Waifs and St rays Society. That was somethingo be proud of.(Applause:) Mr. J. H. SMETHURST, people's warden, said it afforded him the greatest possible pleasure to be presaat,because the past year had been the most successful that he could remember. The ten months that Mr. Bracecamp had been in charge of the parish had marked an era •f progress in the church ,which he very much doubted would ever be beaten. Since Easter the amount of the offertories had been £360 ls.'lod., the amount for the same period last year being ,E3lO, showing an increase of £5O. The wardens found, however, that the more the collections increased, the more funds were required, and they were never "harder up " than they were at the -present moment. That was because so many Costly enterprises had been embarked upon. During. the ten months,they had raised £B3 14s. 9d. on behalf of the Endowment Fund and he hoped that this year the amount collected would be still larger. With regard Oa the needs, of the parish the Church would in time reqiiire redecorating: and re- novating but they had, already £3l ss. 7d. in hand for that purpose. Again speaking of the amount of the collections, Mr. Smethurst said that on Hospital Sunday St. Paul's headed the list of collec- tions at churches .of their own denomination. The speaker alluded to tbe able manner in which All Saints' Mission had been suheriatenied :thy Mr. f Coon ,and pointed out that the present building was not large enough for their requirements. The Sunday School had now to be held in the Church and they wanted as soon as possible to have a separate banding for the school. With that object in view they had already in hand the sum of £lOO 3s. 2d. Mr. Smethurst also spoke of the penny bank,and mentioned that there were 500 regular depositors. Mr. CHORLEY, the Vicar's warden, briefly addressed the gathering. The Vican, at the outset, acknowledged the splendid work which had been carried on by his predecessor, Mr. Tarbuck. He greatly appreciated that work,and con- sidered it a privilege to• follow such a man as Mr. Tar- buck. The Vicar proceeded to thank the wardens for their valuable services and said he did not think two better wardens could be found anywhere in Lancashire. He also thanked the other church officers and the members of the congregation for the support they had g accorded him. Ho came to the parish in fear and trembling at following such a man as Mr. Tarbuck, but he had now realized how willing the con- gregation were to help him, and this mnde his work proportionately easy. The speaker also eulogised the services of the Rev. C. J. Edwards and Mr. Coxon,and continuing, appealed to all present to help him in the fature,as well as they had done in the past. He thought if they did so,that St. Paul's Would progress as much, if not more than ever it had done. in conclusion he proposed a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. Burgess, the secretary of the committee who had had the arrange- ments for the party in hand, for his very valuable services, and to all who had rendered assistance. (Applause). Mr. PARKER seconded, . and the vote having been heartily agreed to, Mr. IlumaEss briefly responded. The entertainment was then proceeded with,and gave the greatest enjoyment to the assembly. All the artistes acquitted themselves in a highly creditable in inner and, several encores were .accorded. Selections were rendered with good - effect by a ladies' orchestra. com- posed of the following :—Pianoforte, Miss V. K. Finney; Ist violias, the Misses M. Leigh, J. M. Finney and M. Chesworth 2nd violins, the Misses L. M. Burchall, E. Greenhough, mid M. Parsonage; mandoline, Miss Mary Ches- worth ; triangle and • castanets, Miss Hilda Leigh. Miss Harrison sweetly and tastefully rendered the songs "Oh, dry those tears" and " Love's bargain " the first soag calling forth an encore. A VALET'S CLAIM. Saquel to a Cycling Accident at Lymm.

B?fore His Honour Judge .Bowen Rowlands K.C., at the Warrington County' Court on Thursday, an action was heard in which James Cawley, .valet to Lieutenant Colonel Cornwall Legh, of High Legh, sued Alfred Read, horsedeller, Stockport, for 250 compensation for . _ _ _ _

injuries sustained it was claimed that tlie defendant or his servants were guilty of negligence by driving a herd of horses along the high road at Lymm in such a manner as not to have reasonable and proper control over them, the result being that they ran loose all over the road and the plaintiff was knocked off his bicycle and sustained serious injuries to his head, faee and arms and his bicycle was dameg ed. The particalars were made up as follows: Doctor's eill l7s. 9d.; extra nourish- ment £2 '; repairs to bieycle Z.l 10s., pa'm suffering and Ldisfigurement for life £39 12:s. 3d.—Mr. Browne repre- sented the plaintiff and Mr. Simms (Messes. Simms and Symes) of Manchester appeared for the defendaat. Mr. Browne, in stating the plaintiff's case, pointed out that oa the 11th of August, the plaintiff was retarn- In, front Lvinin on his biocle. It • was pouring with rain ital It was verydarkat the time. When he reached Higher-lane, Lymm, he saw in front. of him a light , evidently attached to a cart. .11e hod two bells on his bicycle and rang bath. immediately afterwards he felt something come against him. Hewes ; thrown from his bicycle on te :he curbstone and whilst on the ground he was kicked 1.,y a colt in the face. At the tim 3 he was kno3ke 1 off ills bicycle there were a number of horses in the lane' which were not under control. The plaintiff shouted for assistance and a MAR 1111113 i Brooker came up. Mr. Brooker foand Caw- ley in the gutter, the latter being on his proper side. He was in a terrible condition, and he could scarcely recognize him, although he knew him. He recognized him by his voice.. His client asked what the defendant was doing, but there was no answer. Plaintiff asked for the defendant's name and address and the latter slid he had come from Birmingham and was well- known in Lymin. C twley said that there was no one in charge of the animals, and all the three men with the horses were in the van. The defendant refused to give his name and address, but, fortunately, Brookerott the request of the plaintiff, followed him into the village and communicated with Sergeant Sharp, who saw the defendant and • asked for his name and address. He gave it as,Williaur Smith, of NewcaStle-on-Tyne,horse dealer. There waS no 11l inc on the van. The defendant told the sergeant that he was taking theanimals to Chester fair, and the latter replied that he should report the case, as the defendant had no name on the vats, The p )lice were three or four; months before they discovered that the defendant ; lived in Stockport, and was named* Read. In November the defendant was brought up at Lynim Sessions and convicted for having no name on the van ; he practically admitted that he had told a lie. to the complainant and Brooker. The complainant was taken ton doctoaandhad eight stitches put in his upper lip, his nose WAS broken, and three good teeth entirely knocked out, his hand sprained, and he was also very badly bruised, and was in bed for a week. Mr. Browne submitted that the defendant was guiltyof negligence in not having the animals under proper Con- trol. A disfigurement to a valet was a very serious thine, and was likely to prevent him getting a fresh situation if he required one. The plaintiff was then called and bore out Mr. Browne's statement. He Stated that he had not been free from headache since the accident.—Cross-examined by Mr. Simms, witness Said there were more than four horses and the animals were not tethered together. - John Brooker, a waiter, residing at Higher-lane, Lymm, said he was proceeding home on the night in question when he met a dark object en the footpath which afterwards proved .to be a colt. There were about twenty colts all over the road, and he saw no one in charge of them. There wasa van at the back with a light attached. Witness afterwards heard a shout and on turning back saw the plaintiff and his bicycle lying in the gutter. He knew Cawley, but could hot recognise him on this occasion as his face was in a shocking condition. The plaintiff and witness attempted to get the defendant's name,but as they did not succeed, Witness followed the ,van to Lymm and told Sergeant Sharp.—ln cross- examination witness said there were some horses in front and some behind and others around the van. William Hollingworth, Higher-lane, Lymm, a joiner, spoke as to meeting the van about 50 yards from where the accident took place. There were horses on the footpath on which he was walking and horses .in front and behind the van. More was no one in' charge of the horses. It was pitch dark and raining heavily. Cross-examined : He did not see the accident, Sergeant Sharp, stationed at Lymm, stated that he saw about half a dozen horses in front of the van. Witness -Old the defendant about a complaint he had received,and the defendant replied "the man is not hurt. I assisted him on his bicycle and he went on." Witness said that was not very satisfactory and asked him for his name and address. He gave his name as "William Smith. of 48, Fydel-street, Newcastle- on- Tyne." Witness looked for his name and address on the van but found there was none painted thereon. A summons was issued but it was found that the address was a false one. Four months later he discovered that the defendant's name was Alfred Read of 69, Old-road Stockport. The defendant was brought before the magistrates at Lymm and was tined 20s. and costs. Dr. De Jong, medical practitioner at Lymm, des- cribed the injuries. .When the plaintiff came to his surgery he found him suffering from contused and lacer ated wounds. The bridge of his nose was broken and his cheek was badly bruised ; his lip was cut through and the wound extended to the nose. The chin was bruised and the right wrist sprained. Some front teeth had been knocked out. The plaintiff was very weak in con- sequence of the accident, and must have suffered great pain. It was quite likely that he had hardly been free from headache since.—Cross-examined : The injuries could hardly have been caused by the plaintiff falling from his machine on to the curbkone. He thought one of the animals must have caught its hoof on the plaintiff's face. • Harry Dennett,; licensee of the Bulls Head Inn, Lymm, stated that the defendant visited his house after the case had been heard at the Sessions. The defendant told him he had 30 or 40 colts in his possession. He expressed surprise that' witness had not heard of an accident to. a cyclist on the night in question. Mr. Simms, said his defence was a contradiction as to the number of the horses' and he denied that they were loose. The defendant himself had two horses tethered together and another man was leading ,the other two. They were walking along the road, . and there was ample room for a bicycle or a cart to pass. They saw some one come up on a bicycle, and shouted to him to mind the horses. He had no sooner called Out, when the plaintiff and his bicycle struck the off horse, and the bicycle swerved round from the horse and went into the curb, where the plaintiff fell.

His Honour said that if Mr. Simms could show any- thing approaching the state of affairs existing accord- ing to his statement, then there was no negligence. Alfred Read, the defendant, bore out the statement and said there were only four horses in addition to the one drawing the van. Witness was leading two of them tethered together and there was ample room for even a waggon and horses to pass him. Whew he saw plaintiff approaching he shouted "Heigh up!" but plaintiff rode on in a zi;!-zag fashion, and ran into one of the horses. This caused him to fall against the curbstone. Thomas Houghton; Guide Bridge, who was driving a horse and trap and witnessed the accident, corroborated. He, however, said there were five or six horses in -addi- tion to the one drawing the van. Cross-examined: There were mor* horses behind the van.

George Cook, who was leading two horses, and Peter Ford also gave evidence. His Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for the full amount claimed with costs.

NIITE3 EY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

Lii): A CORRESPONDENT].

I dan't think Dr. Latham showed very good taste in trying to re-open the question of Lord Newton's gift to the Council in connection with Newton Common. Pro- ably he was withln his strict rights in calling attention to what he considered was an in,orrect wording or the resolution of thanks for the gill In giving publicity to a nutter of this kind, there are other things to be considered besides the correctness or otherwise of the wording of a resolution. It would never do for the re- lations of the Council with Lord Newton or his agent to become strained, which might very easily happen through the haphazard remarks of a councillor. The Council May hive to go to Lord Newton .again for something and it is just as well to keep on the best of terms with him.• is to be hoped, for the good of the township, that Dr. Latham will reconsider his decision and leave the resolution itl9ne,

The Small-pox patient has not yet been removed from hi :3 home, for the simple reason that the Council have no suitable place to ramove him to. Marquees have beeo ordered, to :be used as a temporary isolation hospital, but it is just possible the man may have be- come convalescent before the place is erected. It is nearly five years since the question of an Isolation Hos- pital was before the Council, and although there have been numerous infectious cases, the Council have not until nowstirred themselves towards providing one. The case is something like the old adage of " locking the stable after the horse has gone." They have thought about providing a hosnital for infectious cases, and now thatacase needing Isollatioa has cropped up, they have nowhere to put it.

Whether or not the Railway Company will act in ac- cordan3e with the sugvstionof the jury at the inquest on the boy who was killed on the railway at. Bridge's Crossing, it would be a sourca of delight to many parents if the crossing was closed and a bridge put over it for foot passengers.

FOOTH A:LT..

Unless the committee of the Earlestown club can put before their patrons better sport than they are at the present time doing, lam afraid there will have to be an enforced sale of assets and there will very soon be a good football field to let. The constant chopping and changing of the team is having a very deleterious effect upon the players, and as a consequence the spectators who turn up week after week to witness the game are becoming disgusted with the sorry exhibitions given of the popular game. On, Saturday the visiting club was Staleybridge Rovers, the contest being for points in the Lancashire League table, the position of Earlestown being an unenviable one. Two new players were tried, a goal keeper naMed Swift, and a forward named Cox, but both were on Saturday's form failures. Cox might be worth another trial, for there is not the slightest doubt he was on one or two occasions spoiled by Raft- ley. The play of the latter is perfectly in- describable. When competing with St. Helens Rees, he showed that he knew how to play footbalL•but since then he has acted more liken school- boy, in fact many schoolboys could give him a lesson in football. At the present time he appears, for some reason or other, determined to show the spectators how not to score although he has had excellent oppor- tunities to shine. If the Earlestown Committee could get hold of a dashing centre forward, who knew his • business, it would pa., them to drop Bartley entirely. ' On Saturday he simply neglected his opportunities and as a consequence Earlestown, who really had all the game—thanks to their clever half backs,—had to suffer defeat by 2 goals to 1. One of the goals was most certainly due to the way in which the goal keeper cleared a dangerous shot, and before he got back between the sticks the ball was shot in. . Such an exhibition of goal keeping has not been seen on the Earlestown ground before, nor do the spectators want to see the like of it again.

As f have said before the committee will have t 6 use more judgment in their selections, or the days of the club are numbered, so far as the public support is concerned.

The Newton club also received a towelling on Satur- day. They looked very much like winning against White Star Wanderers up to half time, when the score stood Newton 2; White Star 1 On changing ends, how- how, White Star reversed the order of things, and Newton never had a look in, the game ending in a win for White Star by 4 goals to 2.

INSURANCE COMPANY SUED. --- A Warrington County Court Case.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday. 1\ lary Elizabeth Kinsella, a widow living at 4, HaliL:-street, brought an action against the British Natural Premium Life Association Ltd., Ludgate Hill, London'to recover ,f2lO 10s. alleged to be owing to her under a policy of insurance issued on the life of her son Thomas Kinsella by the National Guardian Assurance Company, whose liabilities and responsibilities, it was stated, had been taken over by the defendant company.--Mr. Steel appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. T. R.Dootzon,of Leigh, represented the defendant company.—The case had been before the local magistrates on two occasions but had been dismissed on technical points. Briefly stated, the plaintiff's case was that her son Thomas was insured in the National Guardian Assnraneo Company for the amount named. He died in August last, but the money had not been paid. Plaintiff received a circular in April stating that the British. Natural Premium Life Association undertook the res- ponsibility of payment of the sum of ten guineas sub- ject to the conditions appertaining to policies issued by the association. Plaintiff admitted that for a number of weeks she did not pay any instalments, but this was due to the fact that no agent called upon her. During that time she could not find out where the local office of the company was. No notice of lapse had been served upon her. Mr. Dootson's contention was that the plaintiff did not make any payments for sixteen weeks and notice of lapse was served upon her. Consequently nothing was due under the policy. He also contended that after the lapse of the policy on agent of another company went to the plaintiff and offered to place the children insured in the Guardian Company on immediate full benefit in his company if she would transfer them. Ten guineas were paid to the plaintiff in respect of the transfer of the policy in question to the new company. Mr. Steel said the plaintiff denied having received any money at all.

His Hoiiour asked for evidence with regard to the serving of the notice of lapse, and. Mr. Dootson called George Bate, inspector of the British Natural Premium Life Assurance Company, who stated that on the 23rd June he posted a notice of lapse to Mrs. Kinsella. She was then 16 weeks in arrears.-r-Cross- examined by Mr. Steele. The counterfoil produced gave Mrs. Kinsella's addre,s as "Holyfare-street." -John Stevens, Legh-street, insurance agent, stated that he remembered writing out the lapsed notices and posting them. The counterfoil was produced.--Cross- examined by Mr. Steel : He tried to find Halifax-st.. but could not. In the counterfoil the street was written "Holyfare-street," His Honour held that. on the evidence before him there was r proof of lapsed notices having been served. Mr. Doofson said he would call Patrick •Frain to provethat the plaintiff had received ten guineas from another company, to which she transferred the business. The witness was in court but stated that he had nothing to do with the case and refused to go into the witness box.

His Honour said he would sign a subpmna, to be served on Mr. Frain in Court, if he did not go into the witness box.

Mr. Frain then went into the box, and inanswer to Mr. Dootson stated‘ that he was an agent of the United Provident insurance Society. His Society took over the business from Mrs. 'Kinsella. Six children were transferred from one society to another. He gave full benefit to them, and oil the death of Thomas Kinsella he paid the plaintiff ten guineas. His Honour said the plaintiff had been a party to the transfer of the business of the society and therefore could not claim from the defendant society. Mr. Steel observed that he did not know of the transfer. Mr. Dootson said he would not ask for costs. Judgment was then given for the defendant company without costs.

Early on Monday mornin;;;;i. man named Jack- son, employed its foreman painter at Messrs. Richard Evans and Co.'s collieries, Haydock, and residing at Crow-lane West, Earle,stown, died very suddenly whilst proceeding to his work. Deceased, who was cycling te Haydock, dismounted to inflate the tubes of his machine, and whilst doing this he was seizNl with a fit and ex- pired in a very short time. . The wife of the 'deceased lies at death's door. Mr. Jackson was very well-known in the district, especially amongst Conservatives, and although he really took no active part in the fighting department of politics, he was a member of the Con- servative Association. In Building Society circles he was also well-known, having been a director of the Newton Building Society for several years. His sudden demise came as a great shock to his ninny acquaintances and friends, more espcially as he was a hale and hearty man, with no indication that he would come to se sudden an end. He leaves a wife and four children.

SKATING AT NEWTON LAKE.

The severe frost last week rendered the surface of Newton lake in splendid condition for skating, and con- sequently hundreds of people congregated there from the immediate neighbourhood and from the district round on Saturday afternoon. There were several races organised, and the sport was very keenly enjoyed. Although in places the ice was rather treacherdus,there were fortunately no accidents from the breaking in of the ice, but two or three of those who were disporting themselves met with injury from falls. One young fellow' named Percival living at Newton sustained concussion. of the brain, another had his shoulder dislocated, and s young man from Golborue suffered a broken leg. On Sunday a disappointing thaw set in, but this did not deter plenty of adventurous spirits from going on the ice, and although it was covered with about an inch depth of water, they endeavoured to enjoy themselves as well as they were able.

Death of Mrs. C. Rathboae. We regret to record lie death of Mrs. Charlotte Rathbone, of 11, Arpie-y-street, which took place on. Saturday afternoon. Mrs. Rathbone, who has resided in Warringtolj for over 50 years; was the widow of the late Mr. James Rathbone. and the daughter of Mr. William Di Ain. of the Grange Wein, Salop. She was in her 67th year. For about three years she had been in failing health, but her illness took a more acute form about two months ago, when she was con- fined to her house with cancer. The deceased lady was a member of the Women's Liberal Association, and was also, for many years a prominent worshipper at St. Paul's Church. She leaves a daughter (Mrs. H. Miller). The funeral took place at Stockton Heath on Monday. Prior to the interment a short service was held in St, Thomas's Church, conducted by the Revs. H, V, Pigot and W. Bracecamp. INFLUENZA INCREASING.

The Influenza Epidemic is again spreading over the Country. The visitation is of a very prostrating nature, and the disease is certainly on the increase. The origin of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it about is as much a mystery as ever. You cannot trifle with Influenza. It undermines the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost any other disease, and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who imagines he has only a mild form of Influenza, and can go about the street just as usual. It can hardly be doubted that the prevalence of Chest and Lung Complaints must be ascribed to the baneful influence .of Influenza, whose after-effects so frequently take this form. It was a bad day for this Country when this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort its tribute of human life. Having observed its ravages, and its dreadful effects upon the constitution on the occasion of its former visits, we cannot but regard its recurrence with much fear, as a great many have not yet, if they over will,regain their former health. As a preventative, we know of nothing equal to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. A few strong doses taken in time have often provedeffectual in ward- ing off attacks of this Malady, and always succeed in mitigating their severity when a patient is under their influence. Do you suffer from Chills, alternating witty Sudden Flushes. Severe Head-ache, accompanied by difficulty of breathing, and symptoms of Catarrh, Pains in the Chest, under and between the shoulders, and a stiffness and soreness of the Muscles, Loss of Appetite and Nervousness. If you suffer from any of these symp- tom, know that they are the forerunners of influenza, and it behoves you to resort at once to a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. By common consent Patients everywhere admit that they feel more depressed and miser- able after an attack of Influenza than while under its influence, and more than they feel after an attack of 4itly other illness. The best way to banish these miser- able feelings is to take Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic. We can strongly and confidently recommend this preparation, as it has treated severe eases of Influenza, Heavy Colds, Extreme Weakness, and Langour, with greater success than any other known Remedy. We may also add that as a Remedy for Indi- gestion, Sleeplessness, Low Spirits, Nervousness. and Loss of Appetite, Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters stands uneValled. PENKETH.

OLD FOLKS' TREAT.—The annual treat to the old people and widows of Penketh, Sankey, and District was held in the Wesleyan School on Friday last. First inaugurated in 1837, the institution has been upheld without a break since, and has long since exceeded the bArris of the original scheme.. .The subscribers are the people of the district, and they have the satisfaction of knowing that their bounty is fully appreciated by the old people, who discuss the great eveat for weeks before and afterwards. It is said that one old lady on leaving after one of the treats exclaimed in a hopeful tone, "Ah well, it's only 52 weeks awl then we'll have another one." This year several new names have been added to the ommittee, and their efforts last Friday show that the treat will not be allowed to fall behind any of its predecessors. This year 90 people tookadvantaga of the Invitation, and several others owing to ill health and other causes were unable to be present. A bus conveyed those who lived a distance away to the school and was in attendance afterwards to take them home again. A substantial tea was the first item of the proceedings, and tastefully prepared, the old people partook heartily, their wants being anticipated by a bevy of young ladies. After tea the people were accommodated with seats and a varied programme was gone through, the artistes being members of the Warrington Entertaining Society. The following are the names of the artistes :—Messrs. Ireland, Warburton Fieldhouse, Freeman and Kendrick; the latter giving a banjo solo. The laughing songs of Mr Mc Kerritt, and the humorous sketches of Mr. E. A. Saunders, were greatly appreciated, and Mr. J. Emson in his humorous songs was an especial favourite, A novel feature was Mr. Bickford's marionette show, and it quite took the fancy of the nadience. Mr. James Woodward also showed some entertaining views with his lantern, Mr. %V. Kendrick wtts the accompanist. Owing to the length of the pro- gramme, encores had to be dispensed with, though the old people were unstinted in their applause and appre- ciation. After the concert, the chairman of the com- mittee, Mr. George Thompson, addressed the gathering, and wished them . all a happy New Year. He wished to thank the subscribers for the generous way in which they had • supported the treat, and was sure the, old people had enjoyed themselves. There were many changes since he had been on the committee, and he was now the oldest member on it. He spoke feelingly of the absence, through death, and other cans s,of many familiar faces, but hoped those present would be able to be there again next year. H 9 then proposed a vote of thanks to the subscribers, the members o! the War- rington Entertaining Society, and the ladies who hafts° kindly taken charge of the tea. Mr. J. I. .Ambler seconded the proposition which was carried with ap- plause. A move - was then made to the other room. where a capital supper was spread. In leaving each lady was presented with a packet of tea, coffee and sugar, and the men received a pipe, and package of tobacco. Those unable to be present, would have their gifts sent on to their homes. Mr. Thompson remarked that there was a small surplus which would be distri- buted during the weather to relieve cases of distress in the district at the discretion of the committee. The committee this year is as follows :—Messrs. J. Thomp- son (chairman), R. Routledge (treasurer), Woolsten- croft, Ambler, Ireland, Hampson, Gorse, and R. Hough- ton, (hon. sec.) The tea was prepared by the following ladies :—Mesdames Owen, Woolstencroft, Clarke, Wig- field, Routledge, Hampson, Dumbill, Ireland, and Withenshaw, and Misses Tunstall, Houghton, Ambler, Gilbert, Dodds, and Owen, were in attendance ah the tables. Altogether the treat this year can be compared favourably with any throughout the country, and its popularity shows no sign of waning.

The trial of" Colonel "Lynch, M.P. for Galway, on a charge of high treason, opened on Wednesday in the bordChief Justices Court, with all the quaint formali- ties proper to so unusual a case. The prisoner was charged that being "movedand seduced by the instiga- tion of the devil,' he gave "aid and comfort to the King's enemies during the war." Evidence was glycol' of Mr. Lynch's connection with the Irish Boer Brigade.

Mr. Jtilian Ralph, the well-known American aiithor :mid journalist, died in New York on Tuesday night. Death was due to dropsy, brought on in the first instance by his experiences as a war correspondent in South Africa.

COMMERCIAL FAlLURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercvntile Gazette," the number of New Bankruptcy Proceedings in England and Wales gazetted during the week ending January 17th, was 78.
