MUNICIPAL TRADING. THE ADJOURNED COUNCII, MEETING.

A FRESH RESDLUTION CARRIED. An Inquiry by Accountants.

The municipal trading question was once more debated at the adjourned meeting of the Warrington Town Coun- cil which was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening. It will be remembered that the last monthly meeting was brought to an abrupt termination owing to the sudden illness of Councillor Baird. At the time, the Council was engaged in discussing a motion by Alder- man Monks with regard to the proposed inquiry into the trading transactions of the Corporation, but no vote was taken. There was a large assembly of the general public at the meeting on Tuesday, although the attend- ance was not quite as large as on previous occasions, this being due to the fact that in order to prevent overcrowd- ing only a limited number were admitted.- The Mayor's appeal to the audience to refrain from audible expres- sions of their feelings had the desired effect, the de- bate being followed quietly but with none the less in- terest. After considerable discussion an amendment proposed by Councillor Trstitox was carried with one or two alterations, but Councillor PF.MBERTON inti- mated that the form of inquiry resolved upon would not be acceptable to the Non-Trading Committee. It was ten minutes to eight when the meeting terminated. The members and officials of the Council present were :—The Mayor (presiding), Aldermen Francomb, Hutchinson, Monks and Smethurst; Councillors Burton, Bennett, Brophy, Burton,- Davies, Duncan, John Evans, Ellison, Hulse, Lord, Peacock, Pemberton, Pierpoint, J. Roberts, F. R. Roberts, William Roberts, Rogers, Tinnion, Upson, and Wilkinson; Messrs. J. Lyon , Whittle (Town Clerk), S. Wilson (deputy-Town Clerk), and J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer). i The resolution by Alderman Monks read as follows: i —"That the resolution proposed by Mr. Councillor I Dodds, and passed at.the last meeting of the Council, ,be and is hereby rescinded. That in order to give an 1 opportunity for investigating the charges recently uncle as to the trading that has taken placebetweer the Cor- poration and members of the Council during the three years ending 9th November, 1901, a County Court judge or other qualified legal gentleman of equal standing, be selected and appointed by the Mayor, in consultation I with the Town Clerk, to inquire into and report upon any charges submitted to him by the Municipal Non- Trading Association or any ratepayer of the borough." THE MAYOR REQUESTS SILENCE FROM THE

PUBLIC. COUNCILLOR BAIRD'S ILLNESS. THE QUESTION OF THE TRIBUNAL.

At t'ne outset the MAYOR said :--Before commencing the p •oceelings I would very briefly refer to the sad inciaoat which interrupted our meeting this day fort- night when Councillor Baird was overcome by the heated atmosphere and the protracted sitting. I am very glad to know that he is very much betterand I am sure we all 'wish that he may soon be restored to health and be able to resume his place at our Council and committee meetings. (Hear, hear.) While on this subject I would earnestly impress upon our friencLs,the public, to abstain from giving expression to their feelings of either approval or disapproval, as the case may be, of the pro- ceedinri's of the Council. We are very pleased that the ratepayers and the public generally, should take an intelligent interest in our proceed- ings, but there it must end. I would also beg our friends, the reporters, to ignore any accidental expressions of - satisfaction, or the reverse, that may be indulged in at the back of the room, for it is not supposed to be heard, and if it comes to my ears might be under the painful necessity of asking those who indulge in it to leave the room. With regard to the resolution on the agenda, I should like to say that the suggestion of giving to me and the Town Clerk the responsibility of choosing a gentleman to superintend the proposed inquiry, did not emanate from me, and was not even known to me before it was pat on the notice paper. And t may say it is a responsi- bility I should hesitate to accept unless with the advice. assistance, and concurrence of the General „Purposes Committee, or any other committee the Council maysee fit to appoint for that purpose. I think that when our last meetin!; broke up Councillor Pemberton was speak- ing, and I will, therefore, invite him to resume his discourse.

COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON REPLIES TO ALDERMAN MONKS.

WHY' THE RESOLUTION IS NOT ACCEPTABLE TO THE NON-TRADERS.

Councillor PEMBKRTON then commenced the discus- sion. Quoting from Alderman Mon!cs' speech he said the Alderman remarked that he would have agreed to, and welcomed a fair and reasonable inquiry, and he chargedthe Non-Trading Association with being alone responsible for the failure of not having an inquiry at the end of 1001 or early last year. Considering the Non- Trading Association were not _represented on that Council,he failed to see how Alderman Monks could charge them with failing to obtain that inquiry. Several Cenncillors brought resolutions forward, but Alderman Monks did not do so, and he (Councillor Pemberton) had not noticed his name in the reports as supporting very vigorouslyan inquiry inany form whatever. There fore he thought his remarks in that respect were quite uncalled for. Alderman Monks recognised, as he told them in his speech, that this trading was wrong so long as nine years ago. Yet he did not make any vigorous attempts to put it down in the Council chamber. It was only with regard to himself and his firm. He (Councillor Pemberton) considered Alderman Monks had a duty to perform to the public because at that time, irregalar trading was going on. In fact by inquiries which had been held in the Council Chamber he thought it had been conclusively proved to the public that irregularities had gone on. One glaring case was that of the Warrington Electric Light and Power Company, when a tender was let for £7OO without being advertised for. That was against the standing orders of the Council but Alderman Monks did not bring the matter before the Council. Alderman Monks went on to say that "other members of the Council have held the same opinions in regard to this trading question, notably the members of the Finance Committee. This was the one committee which could do more to deal with improper trading than any other, and I must say my colleagues have, in my opinion, risen to the occasion, and before the Municipal Non-trading Association was formed, or its promotors were heard of in Warrington, the committee had anticipated and taken active steps to, improve the situation. Let me point, out, however, there• was no public opinion on this ques- tion until after thedisclosures in Manchester in October and November, 1900, and in the absence of .such a strong opinion as now undoubtedly exists, it -was very diffi- cult for the Finance Committee to take a strong line without running the risk of possible misunderstaadings with the committees and members concerned. To have taken any step or adopted any course of action likely to leail to such a result did not, appear to us likely to help or facilitate the general business of the Corporation, and so, at the risk of the work taking longer to complete, the Finance Committee thought it better to try and bring about the reform by quieter methods. By a word here; a suggestion there, occasionally referring accounts back again with recommendations, and gentle but steady pressure all round, the committee had brought about such reforms as to put matters upon Ault I still think, and (,•ttn only describe as a satisfactory footing. So that when the disclosures were made in Manchester we could congratulate ourselves on having already accomplished what Manchester then found it necessary to do." Continuing, the speaker said that other members of the Council went further than Alderman Monks. Councillor Bennett,as Mayor's anditor,made a report, and he would call to mind the bitter attacks that Councillor Bennett had had to stand in that Council Chamber, with very few supporters indeed. With regard to Manchester, however, Alderman Monks's opinion was that it was not necessary to hold an inquiry because he held that they had already accomplished in 1900 what Manchester had done. That was a most surprising statement to be made in that Coun- cil Chamber. It was since then that these disclosures had come out and since then that trading by councillors privately and through limited companips had been exposed. Then, again, Manchester did notlave Council meetings like they had, and rescind a resolution which had been carried in a perfectly legal manner. Man- chester was a long way in front of Warrington in that respect, because without any outside pressure, when a charge was made, a committee of inquiry was immedi- ately appointed to inquire into the charge, and if those charges were proved, in some eases even if the offence was not illegal, but improper, members hadhad to retire, although legallyithey were not compelled to do so. The law was not different in Warrington to what it was in other places, but the feeling in Manchester was that a member who traded with the Council should no longer act as a members of the Corporation. (A voice: Not correct.) Alderman Monks had said that the principle was the same with regard to a thousand pounds as it was with regard to a pettily, in a sense it was, but yet there was a big lot of difference. For instance, if while walking in a lane he (the speaker) had cut a rough walking stick out of the hedge it would be stealing, but he did not suppose he would bearrested. That, however, was very different front a man who brought a lurry and cut down seven or eight trees worth £3OO. According to Alder- man Monks' argument the man would say, " You must not look at me, see what Pemberton has done. He has cut a stick out of the hedge. The principle is the same, von must first prosecute him." (Laughter.) What the Non-Traders had aimed at Was not so much the small trader with a few shares, but those companies th,st, had been specially formed to trade with the Corporation. The Non-Traders did not hesitate to say that companies had been specially formed for that purpose, but the members of the Council who participated in the ad- vantages of those companies said to other members of ' the Council—and the statement applied to the Mayor himself—that they (the other members) were in just the same boat, although they had only a small holding in a company, because the principle was just the same. It was vary nice and comfortable for them to think that, and such contentions gained them a certain amount of support—support which they were all craving for. They wanted every atom of support they could get,and there- 7 fore they wished to drag people into the agitation who

===== 0002601_19030123_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Friday, January 23, 1903.

had never been mentioned. Alderman Monks,continuing, said: "It is, however, contended that the return of Mr. Dodds and Mr. Pemberton is evidence of the ratepayers' desire that an inquiry should he held. While I do not admit this is so, because only a small proportion of the ratepayers were directly appealed to, 1 may be wrong in my opinion." He (Councillor Pemberton) thought that was a very misleading statement because more than half of the electorate were appealed to. The majorities secured were such as had never been recorded before in the wards contested. What more did Alderman Monks want!. There were two old sayings; "There are none so blind as those who won't see, and none so deaf as those who won't hear." (Laughter). It seemed to him that those sayings were applicable to some members of the Council. The Alderman also alluded to the " vague statements and insinuations that had been flying around for which the Non-Trading Association WaS responsible." He con- tended that they had made definite statements in all conscience. He (Councillor Pemberton) had made the most definite charges in public against certain members of the Council. He did not know what Alderman Monks wanted; it passed his (the speaker's) comprehension. Alderman Monks also remarked that he felt so much solicitudeas to the past was out of place, and too mach like trying to lock the stable door after the horse was stolen. Continuing Councillor Pemberton said that stable was a very large one and held a great many horses,and what the public wanted to know was how many horses had been stolen and who had stolen them, so that they would be able to take care and protect themselves in the future. Yet Alderman Monks told theinalmost in the same breath that he believed there was nothing to inquire into, and asked where the line was to be drawn in regard to trading. - Let them have a full and com- plete inquiry and leave it to the public to draw the line. If Alderman Monks was sincere in saying thathe wanted all improper trading by any members exposing, there was nothing inconsistent in supporting Councillor Dodds' motion. Councillor F. R.Roberts had said that he wanted to avoid being made a fool of. He did not know what, Councillor Roberts meant. Another point which ,Councillor Roberts took up,was that the motion stated that witnesses could give evidence on oath. He pointed out that this could not be compelled. That, however, was not making a fool of the Couecillor as the motion stated "on oath or otherwise." C.nincillor F. R. Roberts also.stated that the tribunal had nep.iwer to examine the books. If people would not come for, ward with the books, however, the public would know what construction to place upon their conduct. Coun- cillor Roberts went on to threaten the members that if charges were made against him at the inquiry the persons making them would be Served with writs within 21hours. He (the speaker) had never referred to the question of the writs before, but Councillor Roberts had done SO on two occasions in that Chamber. All he could say was that. ha would not change places with Councillor Roberts if he had the chance that day. As for threaten- ing to serve anyone who brought charges against him with writs, if. on inquiry they (the Non-Traders) found there were any charges to be in ide, they would not hesitate to bring them, in spite of threats of writs: With regard to the insinuations of" shady. things" it was well known in the town that Councillor F. R. Roberts's brother made the observations about them at a meeting. (Councillor W. Roberts: No.) It was a curious thing that at the last meeting when the Mayor said he thought there had been no trading during the past twelve months, Councillor F. R. Roberts immediately rushed to fall in with that view. Coun- cillor Roberts complained that they did not give him credit for what he had done,Courrillor Roberts had not done much. He had attended only one meeting of the Museum Committee last year. The Non-Traders had been done a great deal of injury by members who said their (Non-Traders) motives were those of personal animus. It had been his (Councillor Pemberton's) unfortunate duty to have to charge several members of the Council with trading, but it had not been a ques- tion of personal animus. Alderman Sutton had always been very fr e idly with hini,'n fact in ire thin once he had gone out of his way to do him (the speaker) personal favours, so that he thought once and for all the personal animus argument ought to be knocked in the head. It was a matter of principle with the non-traders and many times it had been painful to have to make charges against people one knew. At the same time if they did not treat all alike they would lay themselves open to the charge of favouring some and not others. He should advise Councillor F. R. Roberts if he did not wish to have anything to do with the inquiry to retire from the Council and enjoy those quiet hours in his billiard room that he had talked about in one of his speeches. ( Laughter.) He (Councillor_ Pemberton) was sorry the Mayor took the position he did at the last meeting because his Worship stood high above any insinuations that could possibly be cast out by anybody. His position when he became Mayor and the number of shares he had were well-known by the Non-Trading Committee !awl by almost every person in the town. He (the speaker) was sorry that the Mayor had thought it his duty to give expression to the opinions he did at the last meeting. it put the Non-Traders -in an awkward position. Of course he was not surprised in a sense. Being surrounded as his Worship was by a .great many of those Aldermen and Councillors wht had been trading with the Corpora- ! .tion—(Cries of "Is that fair"?), to a certain extent it • was bound to influence his judgment. It came as a great surprise to the Non-Traders that there should be any motion to rescind Councillor Dodds' resolution which wis carried at the lest meeting but one. He had made inquiries and he beli wed it was a most unusual thing. Even sometimes in important matters involving an expenditure of thousands of pounds— The Ma.voit:' That matter his been disposed of. The Towa Medi tells me it is not competent to re-open it. The matterfof rescinding has been already decided upon. Councillor PEMBERTON, continuing, said the Non- Traders were charged with driving manufacturers out o Warrington. He knew t) h's sorrow from personal observationsthat it was not the agitation which was driving them out ;it was the high rates. He did not suppose they had driven anyone out with the exception of Lever Brothers but it Stopped manufacturers from coming in. He (the speaker) could have sold land for two very large works, had it not been for the very high rates of Warrington. The Non-Traders were also told that they would drive the best men off the Council. It was said that self praise was no recommendation, but be thought he was entitled to say that the Non-Trading members would compare favourably with the members they had displaced. As there had never been a vacancy in the Council which had not been very readily filled he did not think it was fair to say that they would not be able to get the best men" to come forward whoa they were required. The MAYOR: I don't like to interrupt you, but this is hardly discussing the question. It is going very wide of the mark.

..... Councillor PEM BERTON : lam only answering what Alderman Monks said in his speech. Alderman -Monks covers all this ground. Continuing, he said that as Alderman Monks went into the question and made all these charges, he thought it was only fair that he should answer them. Alderman MONKS: I did not refer to the matter, as far as I know. _ .

Councillor PEM BERTON concluded by siying that the Non-Traders had very carefully considered Alderman Monks' resolution, and they could not see their way to accept it, because they did .not consider it would be a full, complete, and, searching investigation which would bring to light all the irregular and illegal trading which had been carried on during the past six years. COUNCILLOR TINNION'S COUNTER PROPOSAL. CARRIED BY SEVENTEEN VOTES TO FIVE. Conncillor TENSION, in rising to propose an amend- ment to the resolution of Alderman Monks, said he wished to thank the Warrington newspapers—the " Guardian," "Examiner," and "Observer "—for their very kindly criticism of his amendment, and giving the ratepayers an opportunity of weighing and examining the amendment by inserting it in their columns on Saturday last. If he did not see his way to accept the suggestions that were conveyed in the editorial remarks of those papers, probably he would be able to show that he had reasonable grounds for not accepting them. He had decided to make some slight alterations, -not in the general principle, but in some of the particulars of his amendment. The amendment he now proposed was as follows: " That the Municipal Non-Trading Committee be authorized to appoint a chartered accountant to ex- amine the accounts, books, vouchers and documents of the Corporation for the five years ending October 31st, 1901, and that he shall have a right to examine any officials, servants .or workmen of the Corporation with reference to all trading with the Corporation by members of the Council,either as principals or partners, or through limited companies in which they have been or are shareholders, or whether the trading has been done direct or indirect or as contractors or sub-con- tractors, during the said five years. Provided that at the end of three months the Mayor shall appoint a county court judge or other legal gentleman of equal standing to inquire into any illegal or immoral trading which as the result of the aforesaid investigation may be found to have taken place. That the Municipal Non-Trading Committee and any member of the Council or ex-member may be represented by solicitor or coun- sel at the said inquiry, who shall have the right to ex- amine or cross-examine any witness and have access to all documents. The cost of the investigation to be paid out of the rates. The cost of the inquiry to be left to the absolute discretion of the presiding authority." His object in moving the amendment was that there should be an opportunity afforded of coming to some definite conclusion. This matter was of the utmost importance to the town and the Council, and it was de- sirable they should attempt to got rid of it that evening entirely. (Hear, hear). Alderman Monks' motion pro- vided for a tribunal only, and they knew the Non- traders would not prefer charges. Therefore he (the speaker) was proposing the amendment in the hope that he would be allle not only to reconcile the Non-traders stet the ratepayers, and satisfy them too. Alderman Monks's, investigation—afterwards called an inquiry— would not be satisfactory and would not be accepted. It had been declined by the Non-traders. In his present motion he had endeavoured to provide for an investiga- tion first of all, and an inquiry afterwards into the findings of the investigation. He fixed the limit of the investigation to three months. The tribunal would carry with it that which an investigation would not, namely the re- sponsibility of proof; also it would investigate or inquire into, not only what they might find in their present investigation, but also be open to inquire into what had

been previously stated during the agitation, in the newspapers and in the non-traders advertising circular. So far as he was concerned, under no circumstances would he vote for a resolution that provided for an inquiry after the 31st October, 1901. (Hear, hear,) That was the time in which the Mayor had been elected. His Worship had distinctly and specially vouched, that during his tern of otfice, after attending many meet- ings, and making many inquiries and investigations, he was satisfied that nothing of an objectionable, or im- moral, or illegal nature had taken place. He had further ; said that if the Council decided to accept that portion of Couileillor Dodds' resolution that would carry the ' agitation beyond the 31st October, 1901, he should reeonsider his position. He was not going to inquire as to why the Meyor had taken that position but having taken it, it was sufficient that he (Councillor Tinnion) was not going to be a party to pass a vote of censure on the Mayor. If it was proposed that they should pass a vote of censure on the conduct of the present Mayor, there would not be a single men who would support it. (Hear, hear.) They would notice by his amendment that the Non-Traders had the absolute control of the appointment of the chartered accountant. Ho thought it was only reasonable that as they would be to some extent, in fact, altogether responsible for their chartered accountant's report, they should have the sole choice of his selection. The time he intended to give for the investigation was three months, and at the end of that time they would have got over that portion of his suggestion and come to the tribunal. He had bean to some pains! to try and find out what was the legal meaning of the ward inquiry. Most people mixed it up. Alderman Monks started off by talking of an investiga- tion and then about a tribunal and inquiry, and Coun- cillor Dodds did the same thing. Then the " Warring- I ton :Examiner" in its endeavour to improve on his resolution—he did not say it did not sue- I ceed—started off with the suggestion that I the Municipal Non-Trading Committee be author- ised to appoint an accountant who would be empowered to examine the accounts' books, vouchers and any other documents of the Corporation, and further down they stated that "such inquiry and examination to be completed within six months, &c." Fron the reading of ,this it would naturally be assumed that the newspaper in question had mentioned an " inquiry " in the previous part of the resolution, but that was not so. He only drewattentionto these cases ' to show the difficulty of drawing up a resolution that would meet all the circumstances of the case. He was rather inclined to think that many of them had failed to diffentiate between an 'inquiry" and an "investigation." An inquiry was a court in which both sides could be heard. An investi- gation—such as he was prepared to grant in the first place—would allow the!Non-Traders to have entirely their own way. The investigation would be conducted by the Non-Traders, but this was where the crucial 1 difference lay between himself and the Non-Traders. He could not for the life of him see that it was right for those who investigated to be also the judge,jury.and the • accusers. (Hear, hear.) If his resolution were passed he took it that at the close of the investigation, they would have a court presided over by a legal authority, , who would have the right to conduct an inqulry accord- ing to the usual usages. Everyone would be represented. The Non-Traders would be represented, the Town Council , would be represented—through their Town Clerk, he 1 took it—and those who were accused would be repre- sented. The latter was a point that seemed to have been , lost sight of. He had to provide that the accused also , should be heard and represented professionally, and also that any ex-member of the Council who might be. ' brought into the question should also be represented. Some people had asked him why he proposed to combine the two—an investigation and an inquiry. The reason was that he wished to provide for all contingencies. He was,not going to be in the position that After having an investigation it might be found desirable and necessary to have an inquiry. It had been said that after ,an • investigation the thing would be complete. He was rather inclined to think that it would, and after the in- ; vestigation part had been held by the chartered account- ant,the Non-Traders would have come to the conclusion that they had made a good deal more of it than there : was any justification for. He wanted to provide then that full justice should be done, and that accused, accusers, and ratepayers should be equally satisfied. It had been suggested that it might be sufficient that this investigation should report to the Council. He had had some experience of an investigation that ! reported to that Council, and he thought it was not ; likely having that experience, and after the mis-state- ' ments and misrepresentations that had followed it, that he was going to enentirage another investigation by members to report to that Council. There was an investigation. The report was printed with the Coun- cil proceedings and furnished to the members of that Council. Many hard things had teen said of that report and, therefore, he did not think the fact of reporting to the Connell would have the confidence of the public. He was; however, open at any time to defend the report of the committee to which he referred,- but he did not • think it would be desirable to again trust to any investi- gati oes He believed that the appointment of this judge by I worship the Mayor,at any rate, be would one point upon which alj would be agreed. He thought that the Mayor represented not only the members of that Council, but also all sections of the community of Warrington— (Liear, hear)—and that any selection that he might make would have the full approval of the majority of the , ratepayers, and he would appoint a man to take this in- quiry who would have the confidence of everyone. (Rear, hear.) It would be every important tribunal or ' inquiry. Not only were the characters of members of the Town Council at stake, but also the reputation of the town of Wirrington. Therefore they needed something more than an investigation. He might be told that it was not so much a question of illegal as immoral trading, arid that being a question almost purely of immoral trading, an accountant or three or four accountants would be fit and proper persons to pass an opinion as to what would be moral and what would he immoral. If it yeas thought the accountants were capable, surely then the judge or equal authority ap- pointed having the benefit of the investigation by the acconntant, hnvig sat and heard all the evidence, and after having the opportunity of examining that report would be More capable of saying what was immoral. Then with regard to the question a costs and the question whether the whole of the costs should be borne out of the rates, it had been suggested that a sum should be fixed. He was quite prepared to leave to the Non- Trading Committee the spending of a reasonable sum of money so that without cntting or carping they might provide an accountant who would be above suspicion. The judge should determiue, however, who should pay the costs of the inquiry, and if he was capable of decid- • ing the other question he was surely capable of decid- ing this. If he said the whole should go out of the rates, and that these people had been guilty of the "crimes "imputed to them and that, therefore, they should pay, the question would be settled. It might happen In the circumstances that some who were ac- cused wrongly, would be put to cost, and it would be hardly fair for a man Whose character was wrongly tra- duced to have to defend himself out of his own pocket. The costs would be very heavy, but the ratepayers had demanded an inquiry and he did not know which form they , exactly wanted. -He did not know whether they preferred his proposal or that put forward by Councillor Dodds. But he claimed that his suggestion was as likely as any to fit in with the idea of the man in the street and the average ratepayer. They had de- manded a full investigation that would have the approval and confidence of everyone in the town. He would urge that, whatever the cost might be, they should have the satisfaction of knowing that everything was fair and square and above-board, that. everyone should have the opportunity of bearing it, and he earnestly appealed to every member of the Council to come to a fair con- clusion, putting aside all temper and bias and prejudice and pass such a resolution as Would put an end to that unfortunate business, and get rid of a matter that was a standing menace of the true interests of the town, a matter that was destroying the foundations of public life and which was a scandal that demanded their honest and immediate attention. jApplause.) The MAYOR said he thought Councillor Tinnien's ex- pression was rather strong when he said that he (the Mayor) had vouched for every transaction in committee. What he did say was that he had attended all the com- mittees more or lessaind on every committee he attended he found everything- straightforward and correct, and satisfied himself that everything had beeit properly con- ducted. (Hear, hear.) Councillor TISNION said that he understood the Mayor had said that if they inquired into the trading during his mayorality he would have to reconsider his position as Mayor. The MAYOR said his proposition was that if he was asked to submit to an inquiry with respect to the trad- ing of the Corporation during his Mayoralty he would not do sons Mayor. Councillor WILKINSON seconded the amendment. THE FIRST VOTING.

• Councillor F. R. ROBERTS suggested that the Council might vote upon the first part of Alderman Monks' proposition, namely that Councillor Dodds' resolution should be rescinded. Alderman MONKS said he had no objection to the suggestion of Councillor F. B. Roberts, on the under- standing that he did not wish to see Councillor- Dodds' r esolution rescinded and then no further action taken. THE TOWN INJURED BY THE AGITATION.

. Councillor BENNETT said he would like to explain one or two points to make his attitude perfectly clear. He believed the agitation had done the town. of Warrington an immense amount of harm. Outside manufacturers had been prevented from comity, to the town, and also others who might have established themselves, largely owing to its ill name, as the result of this agitation. He did not think it was due to high rates,because the rates were not specially high. If they compared the rates of Warrington with those of other towns, they would find that Warrington compared favourably with many of them. When they considered the immense respon- sibility which devolved upon them of making the old portion of the town, into a new one and an antiquated town into an up-to-date one, then he thought the rates were not extravagant or unnecessarily high. On the other hand, so much abuse had been hurled at their heads that they had got a bad name. Some of the things which their friends had urged,wrire the very kind of instance, there to induce people to stay away. For there were the misleading statements with regard to the bank overdrafts and the state of their debt. There was nothing alarming about them. The overdraft was a matter of pure business and they knew that their assets exceeded their debts by a large amount. When they came to investigate the charges, they resolved themselves into thin air and he did not suppose there wasanother town in Lancashire which had got such a bad name as Warrington. Councillor TINNION : Blackpool. (Laughter.) Councillor BENNETT . I daresay Blackpool has been as unjustly stigmatised as Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Continuing, Councillor Bennett said the towri had suffered from this kind Of thing long enough. He thought they ought to bring it to an end. In order to' do that,they must have a full, complete, and searching investigation. As to Mr. Dodds' resolution he I did not think it was necessary to raise all i the objections which were raised. (Hear, hear.) I He did not think the resolution was such an I unreasonable one. Personally he preferred Councillor Tinnion's resolution. Perhaps it was more business- I like, but there was a good deal to be said in favour of ' Councillor Dodds, and he did not think it deserved all the criticism and misconstruction which those who opposed it had alleged in regard to it. He did not think the Non-Traders intended to nominate two of their own members. He believed they were sensible and business- like enough to get outside business representatives' and if they were chosen with discretion they might get two I men eminently qualified for the position. With regard I to the suggested two representatives of the Council he would never suggest that two members of the Council I should sit on the tribunal, but two suitable men might I be appointed on each side. Councillor F. R. RonEirrs said the Non-Traders would ' vote twice on the question if they also voted in the I Council. Councillor BENNETT : That is only a quibble. If the Nn-traders had named their men it would have been I better. Continuing, Councillor Bennett said his only , objection to Councillor Dodds' resolution was that the inquiry should be composed of five persons. He thought three would be sufficient. If the parties to the dispute exercised their privileges reasonably he did not see why the inquiry need be unfair in any degree whatever. He did not think for instance that the Non-Traders would appoint Mr. Norbury-Williams. Councillor LORD: He would not act. Councillor BENNETT : I did not suppose he would. Continuing Councillor Bennett expressed the opinion that the tribunal suggested by Councillor Dodds might be a very reasonable one indeed. Councillor Tinnion said he wanted to bring, the matter to a conclusion, but if the Non-Traders would not accept his resolution, they would not be a bit forrader. Councillor PEA COCK: They don't want it. Councillor BENNETT said the great majority of Non-tra- ders' charges had been exivmerated,and their attitude extremely unfair. During 'his nine years connection with the Council he had seen very little indeed that he should regard as dishonest. There 'tight have been occasional minor defects, but he believed that the deal- ings of the Corporation had been broadly and substan- tially all that could be possible under the circumstances that governed so large an undertaking. That did not make him fight shy of the proposed investigation; it rather made him court it. He wanted an inquiry. He wanted to havq his character cleared and the characters of his colleagues cleared, and the way to do it was not to throw obstacles in the way but let them inquire as much as they liked. (Hear; hear). ANOTHER VIGOROUS SPEECH_BY COUNCILLOR

F. R. ROBERTS. TWO ACCOUNTANTS REQUIRED.

Councillor F. R. ROBERTS said he should like to say with regard to certain expressions which had fallen from Councillor Bennett, that if Councillor Bennett thought that to give the Non-Trading Committee two votes in the appointment of the tribunal was fair and ; just, his sense of fairness and justice did not commend itself to him at all. Councillor Bennett's own case was typical of the injustice and the unfairness with which the entire agitation had been conducted. Councillor Bennett might very well take this comfort to his soul —that he would be dealt with fairly and squarely by the tribunal, but he (Councillor Roberts) could not take that view because although Councillor Bennett was quite US much,a so-called trader with that Council as he (Councillor Roberts) had ever been, and rather more so, Councillor Bennett's name had never been mentioned in connection with the inquiry. Councillor Bennett's sense of the fairness and justice of those gentlemen might be a very large element, but he (Councillor Roberts) had not the same reason for it,and, therefore, he was much more anxious to guard and protect himself from anything in the nature of bad faith and improper and unfair dealings with members whose names had been dragged again and again through the mud—whose names were once held to be honourable but were now almost a byword in the town, and who were just as conscious of uprightness, fairness, and honesty as he was and any member of the Non-trading Committee could be. (Hear, hear.) He had pointed out again and again to the Cor- poration and the public that his sole interest in com- panies trading with the Corporation was a matter of £5O. That he could prove and had proved in a. court of law. He had never attended any committee that had given his company any contracts, and he had never said a word in furthering the interests of his company in the Coun- cil. That he had proved to the hilt, and what more could a man do? He had satisfied one of his ; Majesty's justices of the truth of what be said. In spite of that his name wa .still dragged forth, he was still charged with obloquy and he was still treated with contempt as though he was it robber or a thief. Naturally his gorge rose when charges were made against him and it was grossly unjust that men who made those charges should say, "sow we will choose the tribunal, and if you won't accept our judges we will have no judges at all." All he could say was that he (Councillor Roberts) would never submit to such an inquiry, and he was quite pre- pared to accept Councillor Pemberton's suggestion and resignhis seat on the Council and adopt whatever inde- pendent course might commend itself to his judgment. He should take independentaction in the matter if any- thing so grossly unfair as that which he had mentioned was perpetrated. Although he agreed with the bulk of the amendment there was one clause which in his opin- ion, vitiated it entirely, but if Councillor Tinnion could see his way to embody the suggestion he had just made to him he would support him. While on that point he would like to say—and he was sure the Mayor would bear him out—that the suggestion that that inquiry should not proceed beyond November 1901 (lid not emanate from any so-called trader in that Council, whose name had so frequently been before the public, but that It was entirely due . to the Mayor's suggestion, and they had simply in loyalty to him supported it. (Hear, hear.) it was not fair for the local papers, one of them at all events, and members of that Council to suggest for one moment that anything that had the appearance of avoid- ing the inquiry should have been suggested by the men ' whose names had been dragged forward. It was not fair; it was not right ; it was not true. Councillor Tinnion said that that investigation would be conducted by a chartered accountant to be appointed by the Non- Trading Committee. That repeated all the weakness which vitiated Councillor Dodds' resolution. (Hear, hear.) What did it mean ? That that chartered accountant (and from his knowledge of chartered accountants in genera] they were an uprightand honour: able body of men, but just as there were black sheep in the legal fold, so no doubt there were black sheep in the accountant. fold) might possibly be the instrument of the Non-Tiading Committee to carry out what he had always regarded as their persecution. What would prevent such a man from accepting any list of traders and any list of crimes which might be put into his mouth by the Non-Trading Committee and pre-

senting that list to the judge for investigation? (A voice: Public opinion). That was all very well, but if the man was going to take the list supplied to him by the Non-trading Committee, if the Council were to be precluded from naming any other members of the Council who were members of limited companies,which had traded with the Corporation, if the County Court judge was to be confined to the examination of charges made by the accountant and put into his mouth by the Non-Trading Committee, they had got the . evil which was embodied in Councillor Dodds's resolution magnified 20-fold. He wanted it to be an instruction that the accountant should not present the cases which he thought ought to ' be investigated by the judge, but that he should report to the Council the names of all members who were con- nected as shareholders—it did not matter to what amount—and officials with limited companies trading with the Corporation. Surely if it was immoral for him (Councillor Roberts) to trade with the Corporation As a member of a limited companyit was immoral for Councillor Bennett or anybody else. Therefore he said that all the names, without fear or favour ought to be and must be presented. If that were not done they would have a one-sided report which wduld simply include the men whose names had been so fre- quently brought before the public. They, and they alone, would be brought before the judge, and although they might clear their character it should be remembered that prevention is better than cure, and the men whose names have been left out of the inquiry would be in another infinitely better position in popular opinion than the men whose names had been included,but who had been able to clear their tharacters front thecharges brought against them. For his own part, he would in- finitely rather prefer to be in the position of being left alone than to go before the court and clear his character not because he feared he could not do it, but because it was far better to be left out altogether than to have to clear one's character. He thought that would appeal to every fair-minded man. If Councillor Tinnion would amend his resolution so that two accountants should be appointed, one to repre- sent the Non-Trading Committee and one to represent the Council—(hear, hear)—then he would be very much in favour of supporting it, and he thought it would meet every case that could possibly be brought for- ward. It would be fair to all sides. It should also be an instruction to the accountant to present the nanics of any members who could be proved to be members of limited companies trading with the Corporation.' The resolution was amended to provide that one accountant should be appointed by the Non-Trading Committee and one by the Mayor on behalf of the Council. Councillor TINNION said he did not claim that that was the most perfect resolution that could be trained, and if in the opinion of the Council, Councillor F. R. Roberts' suggestion would be an improvement he was quite prepared to adopt it. The seconder of the amend- ment had agreed to that.

COUNCILLOR PIERPOINT'S OPINION OF THE AMENDMENT.

Councillor PIERPOINT said they now had something I definitely to put before the Council. He was exceed- ingly sorry that Councillor Dodds was quite unable to be present. He expressed Councillor Dodds' regret, although he was not authorised to speak for him in any shape or form. He would like to have traversed a great deal of what was said at the last meeting, but he wished to avoid personalities because he was anxious to get the matter out of the way. They had rescinded Councillor Dodds' resolution, and that gentleman had declared on behalf of himself and his party inside the Council, and the large party he claimed to have outside, that his motion was the "irreducible minimum." They could take Councillor Dodds' party to a horse trough, they could put their heads in it, but they could not make them drink. (Laughter.) Councillor TENNION : They will die, then. (More laughter). Councillor PIERPOINT, continuing, said the Council could not get them to drink the resolution unless they chose to do so. He wanted to get this miserable busi- ness done with, and he believed that Councillor Tinnion's amendment was an honest attempt to solve the difficulty which they all wished to get out of the way._ It was his intention to vote for it. (Hear, hear.) _ _

Alderman MONKS said it seemed to him tha his 'resolu- tion gave a wider scope than did Co mcillor Tinnion's amendment, but he was not prepared to stand out for his own resolution. If they were prepared to include in the investigation all tmdin,,,then he would agree to the amendment, and he would like Councillor Tinnion'S amendment to read " to inquire into the cases submit- ted,as to the result of the aforesaid investigation."

SPEECH BY COUNCILLOR LORD. A "DUMMY" RESOLUTION.

Councillor LORD said he had been until recently only a ratepayer of this borough. Like many other rate- payers he had taken a deep interest in this non-trading movement, and, although he had joined the committee, the Council might rest assured there was no personal animus in him. If he thought for a moment th it the Municipal Non-Trading Committee were animated by malice or personal animus he would shun them for ever. He need scarcely tell them that he fully appreciated the honour of representing the Town Hall Ward, and recognized the duties of that representation. The electors declared on November Ist in no uncertain manner for municipal .reform . and a satisfactory inquiry. The resolution now before the C,•uncil in Mr..Alderau;n Monks' name, was, to his mind, one which would satisfy only those who were adverse to a genuine, honest in- quiry. Why the limit to three years ? Was there something beyond that he wished to conceal from the ratepapers? Why did he impose conditions which he knew full well that the ratepayers would not accept? It appeared to his (the speaker's) mind very clear that the object ,was to get a genuine resolution out of the way and substitute a dummy. He might tell Alderman Monks that Councillor Dodds' resolution, which was unanimously passed at a special Council meeting, was the gun by which the Non-traders intended to stand It was the, minimum is7l4ch they as a committee an.d ratepayers were prepared to accept. They wanted no mock inquiries. What the Jameson Raid In- quiry did for us in South Africa, a whitewash- ing inquiry had done for us in Warrington. The first part of Councillor Tinnion's amendment was very fair,and would be acceptable to the majority of the ratepayers. In his opinion the second part might not be needed. He did not think the ratepayers wanted a legal inquiry and he was sure they had never demanded it. A legal inquiry might prove very expensive and could serve no useful purpose. If the Non-Traders could not get 1.1 satisfactory inquiry now, they were prepared to wait until next November and again appeal to the ratepayers (who were the masters of the situa- tion) for further support. Bat remember this—that a thorough inquiry must come, whether now or later,de-- pended upon the amount of fear some members hed,that the public should be acquainted of the facts. Councillor PIERPOINT: Suppose the non-trading part of this Council refuse to appoint an accountant. The TOWN CLERK said they could net make them ap- point one, but in that case the one appointed by the Mayor would go on and make his report. • Councillor TINNION, after consultation with Alder- man Monks, said he thought they had arrived at some reasonable result. The amendment which he now moved was as follows That the Mayor be requested to appoint on behalf of the Council a charteredaccountant, and that the Municipal Non-Tradihg Committee be requested to appoint a chartered accountant to examine the accounts, books.. vouchers, and documents of the Corporation for the five years ending March 31st, 1901, and that they shall have a right to examine officials, servants or workmen of the Corporation with reference to trading with the Corporation by members of the Council, either as principals or partners or through limited companies in which they have been or are share- holders, or whether the trading has been done direct or indirect., or as contractors or sub-contractors during the said five years, and shall report thereon. Provided that at the end of three months the. Mayor shall appoint a County Court judge or other legal gentleman of equal standing to inquire into and report upon the results of the investigation and the report of the two accountants. That the Municipal Non-Trading Committee and any member of the Council or ex-member may be represented by solicitor or counsel at the said inquiry, who shall have the right to examine or cross-examine any witness and have access to all documents. The costs of the investigation to be paid out of the rates. The costs of the inquiry to be left to the absolute discretion of the presiding authority". COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON'S OPINION OF THE

AMENDMENT. • ANOTHER RED HERRING.

Councillor PEMBERTON said they could not accept

Councillor Tinnion's saiendnient,for the reason that he stipulated they should appoint an accountant only.. What use was that ?—That was no inquiry. An account- ant came there and examined the books and vouchers. What could he find out? Nothing. The books were kept in very proper order they admitted. Councillor TINNION: He cau interview all the officials. Councillor PEMBERTON asked why shouldhe interview the officials? He would not know anything in regard. to improper trading and who was a director of or con- nected with a firm trading with the Corporation. The accountant was to be the only man to inquire into the illegal or improper trading. This was simply another red herring, and such a resolution they could not accept. They wanted a proper, open, and above-board inquiry, and this amendment was only throwing dust in the eyes of the Council and the ratepayers, and they would never stop while the public were behind them until they got a. proper, full and complete inquiry. The voting on the amendment was as follows : FOR. AGAINST. Alderman Fatirclough. Councillor Bennett lf Monks. Hulse. 2, Smethurst. 32 Davies. 22 Francon' b. Pemberton. 22 Hutchinson. Lord.-5. Councillor F. Itj Roberts. 2) Tinnion. If J. Roberts. „ Barton. Wilkinson, 22 Peacock. lf John Evans. . 22 Rogers. Ellison. Duncan. W. Roberts. Brophy.-17. Councillor TIN-SION: What is the proper course no Mr. Mayor, so that this can be put into operation as soon as possible?

The iNfAYOR : To bring it before the General Pur- poses Committee on. Monday week. Alderman MONKS proceeded to explain about the Warrington and District Electric Light and Power Co., Ltd. Two tenders he said, were submitted to the committee.

Councillor PEMBERXON : I said they were never advertised for. That is my point. Alderman MONKS Two tenders were submitted. Councillor PEMBERTON : It was not advertised, and the Standing Orders have been infringed. Of course we are in the minority here. Alderman MONKS observed that another statement that ought not to go. without correction was that Messrs. Leverßros. were driven out of the town by the high rates. He did not think that statement ought ta go unchallenged. The reason for. their departure was not that one.

Councillor PEMBERTON : The others were negotiat— ing for land with me. The Council then rose.

LOOKED IN THE. GLASS. What a Lady Saw.

A Newcastle paper relates the following experiences of a South Shields lady whose looking-glass proved the means of restoring to her a treasure she had long lost. ; For it revealed to her a distressing skin disease, due to poor blood, the South Shields and Newcastle doctors ; said. But Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, after the Newcastle hospital for the skin had failed to, I' do so, not only cured this, but also restored her lost health. , "For," said Mrs. Curry," four years ago I was seri- f ously ill. 'My condition was deplorable. I was too weak to work, my appetite completely failed and doctors told me my blood was no better than water skin came ott-n blotches which itched so that te ' Ply con

not rest day or night. Reduced to skin and bone, I was. told by neighborrs that I could not live: " But at Low Fell, near Gateshead, a friend advised Dr. Williams' pink pills. They had cured her when everything dlse failed. When I. took them I found& great improvement : I began to get my sleep, my appe- tite returned, my food nourished me, the blotches dis- appeared, and I became as plump as you see me. • That was four years ago, and I have hate no return of illness. When my children ail, half a pill proves an almost cer- tain cure."

It isa fact that skin diseases result from poor as well as from bad blood, as in the case of the lady of South. Shields. Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people (genuine pills, with the full name—not substitutes) give new vitality and make good blood; anemia, con- snmption, the besetting pains of womanhood,, rheuma- tism, paralysis, St. Vitus' dance, and spinal weaknesses have yielded to their tonic power. On another page of this issue vill be found an interesting article on some of the other disorders—especially backaches—which they have cured.

The lady whose experience is related above, live,s at 32, Edith-street, &kith Shields. She persevered with the remedy, the first improvement coming with the second box, complete deliverance with the third.

Death of Mrs. W. Dooley.

The announcement will be with regret of the death of Mrs. Dooley, wife of Mr. William Dooley, Bewsey-road, which occurred at 10.30 on Sunday morning. The deceased lady, who was 67 years of age, had been in a delicate state of health for a long time, and had been confined to the house for many weeks. Her death, which is due to senile decay, had been expected for some time. She leaves several children. The interment took place in the Cemetery on Thursday. Momento of The Coronation.

The Town Clerk (Mr. J. h. 'Whittle) and the Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot) were on Monday evening the recipients of beautifully-framed -photographs of the Friendly Societies' Coronation Committee, taken by Mr. Birtles. The presentation took place at the Town Hall, Mr. George Maybury (chairman of the committee) presided, and besides the Town Clerk and the Chief Constable there were also present:—Mr. :John Haley (vice-chairman), Mr. Thomas S. Steel (lion. secretary), and Mr. David Houghton (hon. secre- tary to the Friendly Society Council).—Mr. Mavbury made the presentation to the Town Clerk, and °spoke of the happy relationship which had existed betweeit the committee and the officials during the progress of the arrangements for the festivities in Warrington. He hoped the picture would serve as a reminder of the occasion.---The presentation to the Chief Con- stable was made by Mr. Haley, who referred to the excellent arrangements made by the Chief Constable, and said that the success of the procession was due, in a great measure. to his efforts.---Mr. Steel and Mr. Houghton also spoke in eulogistic terms of the work of the recipients.—The Town Clerk and Chief Con- stable suitably replied, the latter remarking upon the excellent conduct of the general public in their ac- cepting the inevitable when the King was stricken down with illness in June.

AN ALLEGED ELOPEMENT.

Singular Case at the Warrington Police

Court.

A singular case was heard by Alderman Shaw Green and other magistrates at the Warrington Police Court an Tuesday, when Peter Rowe and Sarah Jane Ashton were charged with stealing a tin box, four photographs, a number of vases, and a quantity of clothing, of the total value of 40s. the property of Thomas Ashton. Mr. Browne, who prosecuted, said the case was a Peculiar one, as the female prisoner was the prosecutor's wife. Ashton was a labourer at the St. Helens Cable Company's works, and at the time when the offence was committed lived at 19, Annie-street. On the 21st of December he went to work as usual, and when he 'returned at night he found his wife had gone away taking the baby with her, and a quantity of clothes and Other things were missing. He made inquiries, with the result that he applied for a warrant against the two Wsoners for theft. The prisoners were traced to alnes, and then to Wigan and on Monday they were apprehended, and brought to Warrington. The female trisoaer had not known Rowe until he returned from "ollth Africa, wher (Rowe) went to enlist the ~Prosecutor's brother,,lPM so got to know Mrs. Ashton. "len she was apprehended she told the police that " If she got 12 months, she would not live with her husband, but wouid go with Rowe again." Prosecutor stated that he had been married eight 'Years' and had never had a quarrel with his wife during this time. On the date in question she left him 'With two children-3,-yd V; and 2,! years. Mrs. Ashton stated -some of the goods belonged ta her. After hearing evidence on both sides, the magistrates dismissed the case against Rowe and bound the woman 'aver to appear if called upon within six months. -

CHARITY ORGANIZATION SOCIETY. shoull There be a Branch at Warrington?

• With the object of considering the advisability of forming a branch of the Charity Organization Society in Warrington a meeting was held in the Council Cham- ber at the Town Hall on Friday. Alderman Monks presided over a good attendance which included a num ber of clergy and ministers. ' The CHAIRMAN said he did not wish it to be under- stood that by presiding over the meetine, be felt com- mitted to any proposals which would be made. Any proposal regarding ' the formation of a branch in Warrington must necessarily interfere to some extent with the charitable institutions already in existence and which had a vested interest in the town. Their claim should be considered and unless by forming a branch they were going to have greate efficiency or have the work better done than it had bee done in the past, or unless the result of the efforts o the branch would be to procure greater economy so as to increase the funds at the disposal of the committe and in that way render greater assistance to the neces sitous people, it did not seem to him that the new plan was likely to be an improvement on the old one. He had received the following letter from the Mayor ; Dear Sir,—l am sorry not to be able to be present at your meeting to-day for I look upon the Charity Organization Society as a most valuable institu- tion and well worthy of our support. If it is deemed desirable to establish a branch office and officer in Warrington I shall be happy to assist with a small subscription, but I think it should be very carefully considered whether the existing organizations are not sufficient for the needs of the town. We have a charity organization society doing good work in a quiet way under the care and supervision of Mrs. Harrison. We have a very energetic police mission in the hands of Mr. H. Taylor, and we have an officer from the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, so we must be careful not to have more organizations than we have funds to be organized. If the various agencies , I have named all work together we may. 1 possibly not require the aid of an addi- tional one -and I think this should be seriously con- -1 1 sidered by the promoters of the movement you are meet- : ing to discuss. Please make my apologies for non- attendance to Mr. Nicholson whose address I should I have been very glad to have, had the opportunity of I hearing and assure him of my very cordial appreciation of the good work done by the society he conies to re-' : present." 1 Lady Greenall wrote stating that she did not wish to decry the Society, but rather failed to see the need of it in Warrington when they had a similar society carried ,on so, very ably by Mrs. Harrison. Letters of apology 1i for non-attendance had also been received from Mr. 1 Robert Pierpoint, Mr. A. H. Crosfieid, Colonel Richard- son, Mrs. P. Rylands, the Hon. and Rev. C. F. Cross, Goionel Buckton, Mr.V.B.Davies and Mr.W. P. Rylands. Mr. C. N. NicHoLsoN, of the London C. 0.5., then addressed the meeting and said he would be the last person in the world to attempt to interfere with any existing agencies for charitable purposes in a town of the size of Warrington. What he desired was the co- ordination and co-operation of all these agencies and to assist in the carrying out of their work by the adoption ' of one form of organization. He pointed out the saving of expense, time and trouble which would be . effected by this means. Mr. Nicholson proceeded to deal with the general question of the distribkition of : charity and said it should be the aim -of all charity to place the recipient in a position of independence and in the way of earning a livelihood 1, so as to prevent him, as far as possible, requiring, assistanceagain. He emphasised the necessity of obtain- ing full information as to the needs and character of the applicants before administering charity. If a local committee were formed it would obtain this informa- tion and supply it to subscribers. Overlapping would also be prevented. In addition to supplying informa- tion the society would also administer relief. He was told that there was a Charity Investigation Society in Warrington; and what was required was to extend its sphere of usefulness. He heartily Wished the work in Warrington success. (Applause.) , Considerable discussion ensued, the Rector moving "That in the opinion of this meeting it is desirable that a provisiotial committee be formed to consider the wis- dom of the formation of a branch of the Charity Organization Society for the Borough.", The Rev. JOHN YONGE seconded. 0 After further discussion, the RECTOR altered his pro- position to read as follows :—" That a provisional Committee beappointed to confer with the Warrington Charity Investigation Society and other similar 'organizations to see how far the existing methods (inquiry, collection of funds, and the distribution of the same) may be improved and made more efficient." This was unanimously agreed to, and the following Committee was appointed :—The Revs. Canon Willis, A. S. Cameron, J. Davies, Father Wilson, John Yonge, and G. H. Carnson, Mrs. Peter Rylands, Mrs. Edelsten, Mrs Clay, Miss Lucy Broadbent; Alderman Monks, Captain , Crosfield, Mr. F. Stott (secretary of the Trades and ! Laboor Council), and the local secretary of the Fores- ters' Society. •

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

NOTICE TO CORR ESP° N DEN I'S.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as A. guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on onz sidc only of the paper and sent in, if potisible,not later than Thursday. Correspondents are strongly advised to writs briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undartake to return the manuscripts which are not used.

Propos3d Band Conttsti,

Sir,—My.l be allowed to offer another suggeition ? this time to brass bandsmen of this town; viz. th:en- deavour to raise the tone of the bands, in more Ways than one, bybinding together to organize a Grand Brass Band Contest preceded by a March Contegt, to beheld either inParr Hall or Viotoria Park, the latter preferred in my opinion. . .

This c;ould be organized either through the I3ands Association, or by two representatives drawn from each band to form a committee. With careful management a contest should be a decided success, the proceeds in this case could be used for charitable purposes. If this was made an annual event I am sure it would be the means of helping to advance the band cause, and giving to bandsmen an incentive to work for during the winter months.

Hoping that bandsmen will ponder over and act upon this suggestion to inaugurate a most desirable object. I remain, yours Scc., FRED PODMORE.

32, Lovely-lane, may state that my last suggestion re United Choir has been acted upon.

===== 0002601_19030123_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Charity Organization.

As the largest subscriber to the Warrington Society, may I be allowed to call attention to a few points of importance. It is well known that the amount raised for charitable purposes in England is practically a fixed sum each year, and any diversion from the existing channels' means a reduction and crippling of good work which is being already done. The agitation in oonnection with Bartholomew's and other London hospitals, which is going on at the present timo shows, this, pretty clearly. Mr. Nicholson in his remarks treats lightly a weak spot in the proposed scheme, i.e., that " all the agencies must have a certain number of officers and agents em- ployed, and a great deal of expense might be saved," Ste. I have now before me the report of the Warrington Charity Investigation Society, and the "great deal of ex- pense" in this case appears to be a total of £2 2s. spent in printing and circulating the report. How much of this would be "saved" by the new scheme is diffi- cult to see, and if all were, how far would this go towards defraying the cost of the alteration? It would be interesting to know'what amount is spent in any similar town where the Charity Organization scheme has been adopted, and what amount is spent in actual relief in the same town?

I have no balance sheet or figures to compare with the Warrington system, which appears to fill all require- ments, so far as its limited funds will permit,and there is no doubt that if any system such as that now pro- posed, is adopted, the funds will to a great extent reduce those of the present existing .one, and to quote Mr. Nicholson's own words a 'great deal of expense " would be incurred. That the Charity Organi- zation Society is in its way a good thing there is no doubt but, so far as I can learn, the establishment and indirect expenses absorb a very large amount, the whole of which would be saved by an xtension of the present existing arrangement. If no such society existed in, Warrington as the one now established in Bank-street I should have no objection to the new one but,as matters stand, it wouldjappear that not only is it quite uncalled for, but it would cripple, if not destroy a very good work which has been done, and done well, without ex- pense, fora great many years:

• Mr: Nicholson state; ihat "when once a donor gives a cheque he seldom knows what becomes of it." He may be correct as regards his own society, but he is most certainly very far from the fact in making such a state- ment as regards the existing Warrington Society; the only money spent is £2 2s. which goes in informing sub- scribers exactly what does become of it. The point raised by one speaker as to its being "principally expanded on poor women" is surely a fault on the right side, if it is a fault, but although it may be ton certain extent true, it is only sufficiently so to mislead those who have no knowledge of the work done with • the small amount available, which is less than one penny for each inhabitant of the Borough. I note that one most important name has been omitted from the provisional committee, Mr. Taylor, the Police Court Missionary,and trust tha., as a matter of justice he will be included in the number,as being the most competent to judge, from personal experience,as to the necessity for such a change and its advantages, or the reverse. THOS. FLETCHER, F.C.S. Warrington, January 19th, 1993.

Sir,—Will you allow me to protest against the forma- tion of a local branch of the Charity Oganization Society on the ground that there is already in existenle a local Society, having similar objects,and to the efficiency of which the Mayorand Lady Greenall voluntarily testi- fied by letters-addressed to the Chairman of themeeting on the 16th instant. It was thought fit to anpoint committee to consiiler whether it was preferable to re- organize existing institutions or to form a new Society, but existing institutions may decline to be re-organized by a Committee in the appointment of which they have had no voice. Everyone will agree with Mr. Nicholson that it is desirable to prevent over• lapping and fraud in connection with charities,but is it clear that the formation of a branch of the Charity Organization Society will achieve that object? Some amusing anecdotes which Mr. Nicholson related to the meeting went rather to prove the contrary. it was impressed upon the meeting that the primary object of the Charity Organization Society was investigation,and the secondary relief. I think our local Society would reverse the positions,and that most people would approve of its so doing. Does a society which exists primarily for the discovery of fraud, appeal very strongly to the charitable? Mr. Nicholson stated that the London branch annually administered £,10,000. at the expense of about £lO,OOO. It is for the charitable to consider whether they would like ss. in the £ of their donations to be expended in administration expenses. The Mayor in his letter to the meeting referred to the Police Court Missionary and the N.S.P.C.C. Inspector, two efficient resident officials who are always reidy and willing to assist in the investigation of suitable cases. Surely therefore to form a costly society, to undertake duties already efficiently and iytexpensively performed, would be an undesirable "over-lapping" of work.—Yours &c., E. AUBREY WHITE. Warrington, 22nd January, 1903.

THE LICENSING ACT. " Sailing Close to the Wind."

John Trenton made his 39th appearance before the Warrington magistrates on Wednesday. the charge against him being that of being drunk and disorderly in St. Austin's-lane the previous evening.—P.C. Casey and P.S. Barnett proved the case. The Chief Constable said that was prisoner's fourth appearance for drunkenness within 12 months but he 'WM not prepared to sly that the man was an habitual drunkard in the literal sense of the term. Prisoner got drunk pretty often but he (Mr. Talbot) took it that an habitual drunkard was one who got drunk nearly every dav and fulfilled no good office in society at all. Prisoner, had, however, been sailing very close to the wind but he thought the Bench might give him another chance.

Prisoner was fined 2s. Gd., the nivistrates warning him that if he wtme before them again for drunkenness his name would probably he placed upon the "black list."

THE_ CORPORATION AND It IV RR POLLI7TERS.

Mr Moulton then proceeded to deal with the deposits of chemical refuse in the Mersey. The witness admitted that the Corporation had had many complaints from the Ship Canal Company about these deposits. Com- plaints had been made about lime being thrown in, and the firm of Crosfield awl Sons had been referred to. They had poured refuse lime into the river for years. That discharge•of refuse lime went on until March,lB99. The only thing that the Corporation had done was to give the firm notice, and to compel them to construct tanks. Notwithstanding this, some lime still went into the river. The firm put up the tanks before 1899 before the action. When there was sewage in the water and lime refuse went into it, a good deal of the sewage would be carried in, but he admitted that lime had the effect of precipitating the sewage and making the deposit sticky and difficult to remove. This was a peculiar kind of lime refuse, and it had this effect in the river. This refuse if years ago used to be dis- charged through the public sewer, hut it was now passed through a private sewer belonging to Crosfields. Cros- fields turned sewage out of the public sewers and into the private sewer. Fifty years ago it was going through the public sewer. The Corporation had given them notice to stay the discharge, and warned them that they would have to put up an extensive nlant to deal with the refuse. The complaint as to Monks, Hall, and Co. was that they discharged oxid's of iron. He did not, however, think that that would precipitate sewage. The Corporation never instituted proceedings .against any persons discharging refuse into the river. The witness was 're-examined by Mr. Brmsfield at some length, and the further hearing was adjourned. THURSDAY'S PROCEEDINGS. The hearing was resumed on Thursday.
