WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL COMPANY. WARRINGTON TRADERS' COMPLAINT.

The Evidense.

In tho Chancery Division of the High Court of Justice on. Saturday Mr. Justice .Byrne resumed the hearing of the action by Messrs. J. Crosdeld arid Sons and other Warrington traders and the Corporation of Warrington against the Manchester Ship danal Com- pany. The plaintiffs claim a declaration that the defendant Company are bound at their own cost to dredge the river Mersey at Warrington to a certain depth. They ask for the right to use the canal free of cost so long as the defendants fail to discharge their Parliamentary obligations, claim the return of the tolls paid in respect of the navigation of the canal, with damages, and apply for an injunation. Mr. Bousfield, K.C., Mr. Hughes, K.C., Mr. Norton, K.C., and Mr. Thompson appeared for the plaintiffs ; 3lr. Moulton, K.C.' Mr. Cripps, K.C., Mr. Pickford, K.C., and Mr. 0. Leigh Clara were counsol for the Manchester Ship Canal Company.

Mr. Benstield read fartner correspondence, consisting largely of the plaintiffs' letters to the Company, com- plaining of innumerable obstructions in consequence of deficient dredging, with notices of the stranding of their steamers and barges, together with details of the car- goes they had had to lighten in consequence of the ob- struction to the navigatiOn. The plaintiffs also com- plained that in some cases'they had to despatch other goods to fulfil their orders. The plaintiffs alleged that in some cases the Canal Company had not sent the dredger at all, but had waited until the winter flow of water in the Mersey had of itself cleared thechannelto the depth of eight feet. Mr. Moulton said the Canal Company maintained,that the obligation stated by his learned friend wab not at all the obligation of the Company, The Company's obligation to dredge could not be an obligation to dredge sa that they were to give the eight feet, the plaintiffs spoke of. The Company's contention was that they had to dredge originally a channel absolutely defined; and after the Original dredging all they had to do was maintenance, and that maintenance was the same duty as the original dredging.' They said they had always done that.

===== 0002601_19030123_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
A QUF,'STION OF DEGREE

Mr. Justice Byrne said it came very much ton ques- tion of degree, whether the Canal Company had with proper diligence done all they could in reason,to keep the channel as it was originally dredged. Mr. Moulton: That is our position. Mr. Bonsfield: That observation only applies to the special remedy of toll free passage.' • Mr Justice Byrne: You mean to say there is an obligation beyond that. Mr. Moniton's case is,thera is no obligation beyond that which he has mentioned. Mr. Moulton: Your Lordship will understand that the fact that there is silting up is no breach, because the duty of maintainingldredging means that there must be silting. The fact that a house is out of repair is not necessarily a breach of a covenant to repair. Mr. Justice Byrne: If you ought to have employed more dredgers, then you have not fulfilled your obliga- tions.

lIIVIDENCE OF THE WARRINGTON BOROUGH SURVEYOR. Mr. Thomas Longdin, borough surveyor of Warring- ton for 27 years, WM then called. Ho stated that the river was largely used by traders at the time of the making of the canal. Under the Ship Canal Act the Canal Company had taken the water of the Mersey to Rixton; they had canalised the bed of the river and made it into their canal. The locks at Latchford and the weirs on the Mersey were under the control of the Canal Company. Twenty-three years ago the height of low water at Warrington bridge wasabout 23 feet. He advised the Corporation of Warrington in connection with the Shipranal Bill. Warrington was very friendly to the bill and helped to promote it. The traders-of Warrington did avary large traffic on the river Mrsey. After the Act was passed,the. Canal Company did not comply with the obligation on them to dredge the bed and banks of the Mersey to a depth of eight feet, and con- siderable penalties were incurred. After the agreement of 1896 the Canal Company dredgeda channel but not a channel eight feet deep at low water and 50ft. wide. Complaints from the traders began not long after the agreement, and in December of that year he made a formal corn- which was succeeded by others, accompanied by plans. Up to 1899 records were kept of the depth of the channel at Warrington. The witness produced a log- book, commencing Ist March, 1899, showing high and low water at Warrington Bridge, the rainfall and wind, the dredgers at work,and the state of the channel. This was supplemented by plans of cross-sections of the ohannel made front year to year, from which it appeared that so far from the channel being dredged to the statutory depth of eight feet in some places it was only three feet deep. At this juncture the further hearing was adjourned until Tuesday.

TUESDAY'S PROCEEDINGS. COMPLAINT AGAINST MANUFACTURERS.

On Tuesday Mr. Hughes, K.C., who appeared for the plaintiffs, said that by the amended defence it was alleged that the dredging of the channel of the Mersey at Warrington had been made very much more difficult by reason of the plaintiffs having improperly and un- lawfully discharged lime and other manufacturers' refll9o into the river. The . defendants said these acts had seriously obstructed them in the discharge of their duties under the agreement. His clients had a large. mass of evidence on that point, but as it was for the defendants to prove their allegation affirmatively, he suggested that they should first ascertain what case the defendants made and that the plaintiffs should give their evidence by way of rebuttal if necessary. Mr. Moulton, ICC., who appeared for the defendants. said the plaintiffs ought-to give their evidence in the ordinary way. Mr. Justice Byrne said the cross-examination •of the plaidtiff's'witnes3es,xvonld show whit the case for the defence was. -

Mr.. Thomas Longdin the borough surveyor of Warri ng- ton. v,-ns further examined. _ _

Has the channel been made and completed to your satisfaction?—No. _ _

Or to the satisfaction of any reasonable man in your position ?—No. !Ta cross-examination by Mr. Moulton the witness said he had been borough surveyor of Warrington for 27 years, and he took part on behalf of the Corporation in , the discussions connected with the original bill and the agreement. Sir John Wolfe Barry made a number of observations of the bed and channel of the Mersey. Bafore the Ship Canal was made there was no getting from Warrington to the sea by the channel of the River Mersey at neap tides by vessels drawing more than 3ft. In 1893 the Warrington Corporati'on came to an agreement with the defendants with regard to the opening of the canal. The agreement recited that whereas the dredging authorised and required to be executed by the Company, could not be completed before the Ist January, 1891, and the Company intended to open a portion of the canal on that date with the consent of the Corporation of Warrington, the Company bound themselves to complete the dredging by the Ist July, 1894, under a penalty of £5O per day, and the traders of Warrington were to have the right to carry goods for the purposes of the business free of charge along the Runcorn and Latchford Canal until the work was completed. The Runcorn and Latchford Canal took water which otherwise would have come to War- rington. In the case of a flood the Warrington Corpor- ation were thankful to the Ship Canal Company for taking the flood waters. Down to Rixton the beds of the Rivers Mersey and Trwell had been canalised. The Corp'oration of Warrington might have had a right of joint controi over Latchford Lock, but they never took advantage of it. He would not like to say that the quantity or water diverted by the Ship Canal in ordinary times was less than what used to be diverted by the Runcorn and Latchford Canal.

Mr. Moulton said he proposed to prove that the Ship Canal Company did not abstract as much water as the Runcorn and Latchford Canal used to get. Mr. Hughes, K.C. : There is no issue as to that. Mr. Moulton said the plaintiff's case was that the levol of low water at spring tides had changed since 1896, and the reason given was the abstraction of water. in 1896 the traders of, Warrington accepted loft., but Mr. Bons&ld, K.C., said the abstraction of water had made a difference. He r.Moulton) was going to prove there was no difference. If his learned friend would admit that his clients did not abstract more than they did on that dste he would be satisfied. (Laughter.) Mr. Hughes said hewas much obliged, but he did not accept the invitation. (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19030123_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
COMPLAINTS TO TILE SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

In further cross-examination the witness was taken through the written notices given by him as borough surveyor to the Ship Canal Company as to obstructions in the rivorMersey and the dredging which he required. His first notice was in October, 1898. Up to that time he had never given any intimation to the Manchester Ship Canal that they were not doing the work to his satisfaction. His. next complaint was in August, 1899. Asked whether he made no further complaint until that time because he was satisfied with what was being done, the witness said he had never been satisfied with the way the whole river had been dredged. Sections of the river had been dredged, but they filled up again. He was satisfied until he found the dredging was not going on, and then he served further .notices. The reply of the Ship CAUfd Company in August, 1899, said that . the Icontractor for the dredging was making every effort to dredge the river ; pointed out that the little depth of water was due to the drought. and the continued deposit of manufacturers' refuse : and said that they were entitled to the Corporation's assistance in preventing the illegal deposit of material in the river. You knew that refuse was being illegally put into the river ?—We knew it before, and weserved notices on every manufacturer. The wittoss added that Mr. Jackson prepared the soundings on which the complaints of that time were made. The hearing was adjourned.

STOCKTON HEATH.

HILL CLIFFE AND STOCKTON HEATH UNITED BAND OF HOPE.—A successful cinematograph entertainment was given in the Church Room, Grapnenhall-road. on Monday evening in connection with this society. The Rev. J. S. Hughes presided over a crowded attendance. The exhibition, which was of a very interesting and amusing nature, was provided by the _committee of the Lancashire and Cheshire Bard of Hope Union, the pro- ceeds being on behalf of the new buildings and temperance teaching scheme. In addition to the numer- ous animated pictures a little variety was introduced by way of illustrated songs. &c. Miss Edith Strange sang "Ora pro nobis," Miss Mounfield " The Children's Home" and" The Star of Bethlehem," Messrs' George and Septimus Moss the duet " Larboard Watch." and Mr, George Whitfield recited "The Fireman's Wedding." These items wereall well rendered and were made most effective by the beautiful illustrations given on the sheet. Instrumental selections, which were highly ap• preciated, were given by the QuintettePartv, consisting of Miss Turton, the Misses Ward, Miss Vane and Mr. Vane Mrs. Hughes ably acted as accompanist. POOR CHILDREN ENTERTAINED.—The members of the Hill (life Young People's Society of Christian En- deavour gave their second annual treat to the poor children of Stockton Heath and Walton, at Hill Cliffe School on Friday last. Excellent arrangements had been made for the entertainment of the youngsters, the members of the!societitP under the direction of the Pre- sident (the Rev. J. S. Hughes). the Vice-President (Mr. Joseph Moss, jun.), and the Secretary (Mr. R. Lloyd Jones), entering very heartily into the work. The school was filled to its utmost capacity. The tables were nresided over by Mrs. Hughes, Mrs. Williams, Miss Moss, Miss ‘Vorrall. Miss Hall, Miss Holland,Miss Jones, Miss Griffiths, Miss Dodd, Miss Bell, Miss Marion England. aided by a number of yonne men. The tables were laden with piles of sandwiches plain, currant, and seed bread. together with scones, buns and other cakes and mince pies, and the attendants had a busy time in meeting the demands of their guests. After tea an entertainment was provided to which the regular scholars in the Sunday school had/been invited, as well as a number of friends who were admitted at a small charge, with the result that at seven o'clock the , schoolroom was packed. The chair was taken by the I President (Rev. .1. S. Hughes), who,afterexplaining the circumstances under which they had met and the object of the treat, asked Mr. Williams to entertain the com- pany with his powerful phonograph. "Records "of ! songs and choruses by the best artistes and bands in England and Americi . were given to the intense delight of all. One of the finest renderings was that of the " Halleluiah Chorus," by a famous orchestra. The Chairman gave a short speech into the phonograph, ! expressive of the thanks of all for the kindness of Mr. and Mrs. Williams, which the children emphasized with three cheers, all being reproduced to perfection by the machine. Interspersed with the selections on the phonograph were exhibited lantern views, Messrs. Cobb and Hale, Latchford, kindly giving their services forthe purpose. On the motion of Mr. Joseph Moss, junr., seconded by Mr. R. Lloyd. Jones. thanks were given to all who had so generously contributed to the treat and entertainment. Each child was presented with an orange on leaving.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETIC'S.

Rugby Football.

By " OValiSt."

The frost was all-powerful Oa Saturday and very few of the regular football fixtures were fulfilled. In fact there were only five Northern Union games altogether, four in the League First Division and one in the Second Division. As usual, Warrington were among the sufferers, and another good home " gate" was lost through the unfitness of the ground. • Those clubs which had got their playing patches.welldovered managed to bring off their engagements, and even such a poor club as Brighouse Rangers managed to defeat the forces of King Frost by the aid of plenty of straw, and the persuasive powers of the Corporation steam roller. There is stole enterprise in such a proceeding as this, and the big crowd at Line Heaal to witness the game with Swinton, doubtless "conpens- ated the " Briggas " officials for their outlay. I wish, as an experiment, Warrington would cover the ground sometime when the winter season lnis set in and let 113 see whether the game is worth the candle. There is plenty of Straw and several steam rollers in the district.

St. Helens should have visited Warrington, and the match would have been an interestiag 0112, eipecially as Warrington had a somewhat experimmtal team. The " Smuts" have to postpone their hour of venc,reance, and I think Warrington will be ready for them when it comes.

Broughton Rangers have soon been deposed from the leadership of the Leagne,not by fralif tx, but by their near neighbours Swinton. The uaexp Ael happened to the Rangers for they entertained a weak (on paper), Oldham team at Wheater's Field and were beaten by 5 points to nil. The display of the home team was shocking, I am told, Wils9a being ab ntt the worst man of the field. Oldh tin were Very energetic, and beating.the_ Rangers_ forward they gave

them no quarter afterwardi. dtlift tyrlaßranghton Rangers each have 23 points out of 21) matchei, while Swinton are at the head of affairs with a lead of two points and 2 L matches prayed. The championship was never in so open a condition, for Swinton, Broughton, Halifax, Salford, Oldham and Rut.corn all have a chance, and the ultimate winners need a lot of picking. For myself I incline to the chances of Swin- ton, especially seeing that they play the Rangers to- day at Swinton and will .probably obtain a further lead.

Batley are another lot who are coming back into form and will probably be prominent once ag tin in the cup-ties. They went to Bradford on Saturday and defeated the home club by 7 points to five points—a bitter pill to Brodley and U-)., who obtained their score before Batley had opened an account. The fourth League match was at Hull where the town club fought a ountrance on the ground of the latter. The Rovers once again gave a dashing display- and quite Outplayed the club to the extent of 10 points to nil.

BEFORE THE PAUSTRATES.

A NEGLECTFLTL FATHER SENT TO GAOL.

George Morrell, a labourer, living at 11, Elizabeth- street, appeared before Alderman Shaw Green and other magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday on the charge of neglecting his five children.—The evi- dence showed that prisoner did not provide sufficient money for his wife and was repeatedly getting .drunk. On Saturday,- night he came home drunk and turned the children out or the house. They wore obliged to seek shelter at a neighbour's. On visiting the house on Monday evening Serg,:.•ant Cheney found the eldest boy aged 13 sleeping in a bassinette. On one occasion in- spntot Hackett wont to the house and found there was neither food nor fire in it.—Prisoner, who had pre- viously been imprisoned for a similar offence, was sent to gaol for two months.

„ ROBBING AN Es PLO Y ti.l2.—Frecierick Clutterhuck, 21. Catherine-street, pleaded guilty to stealing a quantity of timber of the value of As. from the mattress works of Messrs. William Hickman and Son, White-street, on the previous Thursday.—The facts of the case as stated by the Chief Constable, wore that the prisoner had been in the employ of Messrs. Hickman for about eighteen months as a mattress maker. At about 6.30 on Thurs- day evening P.C..Clough was on duty in Bewsey-road and met the prisoner carrying a quantity of timber. Ho stopped him and asked Rim how he Came into possession of the wood,and prisoner said he had a per- fect right to it as he was going to do some work for Messrs. Hickman with it. The officer let him go,but on making inquiries he found that the prisoner had no right to bike the timber. On Saturday, P.O Clough and Detective Forster arrested the prisoner at. the works,and on being charged, prisoner admitted the theft and said he was sorry for what he had done. He said he was taking the wood home to make a mattress for his own. use.—Mr. Hickman expressed surprise that the pris*ner should have committed the theft as he had always been asteady workman. He was willing to take him back into his employ and asked the Bench to deal leniently with him. The, magistrates recorded a con- viction against the prisoner and allowed him to go.

WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY LIMITED.

Annual Meeting.

Mr. Thomas Itirchall, J.P., presided at the annual meeting of the Warrington Co-operative Society held on Monday evening. Amongst those present were : Messrs. W. Jeffray,R. Cook, S. Dunbobbin,E. Williams, T. Critchley, J. Albinson, T. Wadsworth, George Wallington. Crowther, W. Lowe, with Alderman Shaw Green (auditor). Mr. A. H. Rathbone (managing secre- tary, and Mr. W. Ford (cashier). .

TUE PREsIDExr ON Tag SOCIETY'S PROGRESS

The Pay.sinExr stated that that was the 148thTplar- terly meeting in conneetion with the Ca-operative Society in Warrington, and they were entering upon the 43rd year of their existence. He remembered the for- mation of the society; he was then a young Man of 30. The experience of that 42 years had been a valuable .one, and it had brought forth very great results to that society. (Hear, hear.) In the early days of the society they had crowded meetings and many of the members came because they did not know what Was going to happen. I They did net even know whether they were going to Imre a dividend. (Laughter.) The members would see front the b tlanc,i sheet that the society had made an advance in the sales daring the past quarter of .C 3,200. That was a very considerable advance upon last quarter and was very satisfactoryindeed. Looking at the movement generally,he could only say that their progress in Warrtngton was fairly symptomatic of the general advance of the Co-operative movement through- out the country. People Were beginning to realise what a great power it W. 13 and were beginning to take advantage of it. The p tst year had witnessed some curious developments. The private traders of the coun- try seemed to have awakened to the fact that there WAS something in co-operation that was making them afraid. They began to rouse the.nselves and threaten terrible things. Their neighbours at St. Helens, who had made such wonder- ful advancoa, had in spite of the boycott increased their business daring the past quarter by .4.22,000 or £3,000. Tile traders now satiated to have droppA all active op- position and if they were wise they would. The only efflet of the opposition had been to advertise the Co- operative movement and to SD:lle purpose. He (the' President) was in St. Helens last week, following one of the most active members of the St. Helen; society to his last resting place. Tee:a wts a crawled assembly, and the loss to St. Helens would be great. He did not think he had he trd a more worthy tribute paid to any man than that delivered by C mon Willinek on this oc- casion. There was one cause for thankfulness and that was, that as those who had grown grek in the service dropped out of the ranks, there were young men, retdy to step forward and fill up the gaps. There had been a good deal of criticism about the practical beeefits of. co-operation. He thought in one cease there hid been aced tinamount el cynical and unfair criticism. He was not one of those who looked upon co-operativeism merely from its practical benefits of dividend, for it had accomplished a great deal more, but he held in the fore-front the practical benefits that the workingman derived. Let them think for a moment of the immense advantage to the workingmen of this country that the hundreds of thousands of pounds—he might say millions—which were distributed in dividends would be. In Warrington alone they -Would be dividing over ,Cl,OOO as the result of the past quarters sales. A great deal of it would go into homes where there was only 21 a week being earned by the bread winner. By means of the dividend, many were able to make their homes more comfoiteble, to provide more com- forts, and to save for a day when they might be in need. There were members of theirsociaty who never saved at all,and when a man became thriftyit put more back- bone in him and made him look more to the future. The ' thriftless manwtsalways from hand to month and when I the least misfortune-befel him, the community had to keep him. With most of their members that was, not the case. He remernberedhearing one of their members at Barnsley state that during the great coal strike, the I members of the Barnsley Society drew out of the funds £70,000. The members of the Barnsley Society suffered little in- comparsion with those who were not members,and-within three years after the strike,the money which had been withdrawn was put back again. That I was something to makea man independent and if they put more backbone into a man then they were doing a great deal of good. He hoped they Would all think of this great movement and recommend it to their friends. (Applause).

In their statement, the committee state "they are pleased to report that satisfactory progress is still maintained at the Stockton Heath Branch, The sales for this quarter are £1,732 against £1,141 forlast quarter showing a difference of £291 in favour of the present quarter for which the committee thank the members as well as for their appreciation of the movement. The sales for the quarter amount to £32,405 12s. 3d.,showing an increase of £2,002 12s. sd. over the corresponding quarter ofJast year,and £3,201 Hs. 10d. over last quarter, The profits, after providing for all liabilities, deprecia- tion, interest on capital, amounting to £529 13s. 5d., wilt allow a dividend of 3s. in the X, on Members' pur- chases.

The report wasadopted on the proposition of the pre- sident, seconded by Mr. W. Jeffery.

ELECTION OF PREifDENT

The PRESIDENT said the next bUsiness was the appointment of a President. He would now vacate the chair.

Mr. Jeffery took the chair. Mr. ALBINBON moved the ro-election of Mr. Birchill. Mr. GEORGE WALLINGTON seconded, and observed that Mr. Birchall had held the position for 37 yoqrs. He woul dbe glad to see him complete 40 years as their Presidpnt. (Hear, hear.)_ -

Mr. JEFFERY supported the proposition and said they might go further and far worse. The proposition was carried unanimously. Mr. 13rnetn.r.r., on again taking the position of pre- sident, thanked the members for the confidence they displayed in him. Ife•believed his position was a unique one in co-operativeism, for he was told that no other man had held the position of president for so long a time. All the work he had done for the society was work he was thoroughly satisfied with, and would do it again if he had the' opportunity. ALDERMAN SHAW3OREENIRE-ELECTED AUDITOR. N. JEFFERY moved the re-election of Alderman Shaw Green as auditor, and referred to the great inter- est Mr. Green took in the society and the care with which he audited the accounts.

..... Mr. DITNROBBIN seconded the proposition. Mr. WA LLINGTos' supported, and stated that Mr. Green was a man worthy of thoir confidence. After so many statements had,been made against their public men he was very glad to have that Opportunity of saying that he had every confidence in Alderman Shaw Green and several other gentleman who had been so much scandal- ised during the last few months. (Hear, hear.) Mr. RATRBONE also supported the proposition, and referred to the very great pains which Alderman Green took in discharging his duties as auditor. The proposition was carried unanimously. Alderman SHAW GREEN acknowledged the compli- ment in feeling terms.

SUMO DEATH OF THE REV. DR,

PRESTON.

The Rev. William Preston, D.D., died soddenly at Rftskelfo Vicarage, Easingwold, Yorkshire on Friday last. For many years he was Vioar of Yorkshire, Trinity Church, Runcorn, and was held in high esteem by the member of every denomination in the town. Dr. Preston recently had i attack of influenza. but seemed to be recovering, when on Friday morning ho died from heart failure. _

In 1885 the dec.elsed was ordained deacon by the Bishop of Ripon (for York), and in 1886 he received priest's orders. At that One he was curate at Atter- cliffe, Sheffield. In 188° he was removed to the curacy of St. Catherine's,Tranmere, where he remained until 1870, when he came to St. George's, Liverpool. His stay in this city was but brief, for in 1872 he was offered the vicarage of Holy Trinity, Runcorn. There he remained until 1898 when ho became Vicar of Raskelfe, in Yorkshire._ Dr. Preston obtained the

di' roe ok B.A. at Trinity College, Dublin, in 1872, M.A. in 1975, and B.D. and D.D. in 1899. Asa preacher he wan very popular, and many of his sermons have been published. He was the author of numerous works on "Church and State." For many years he strongly advocated the cause of the Society foe Promoting Christianity among the Jews,'his frequent visits to Palestine hiving kindled his enthusiasm in 'the matter. He also lectured a great deal for the Bible Society. For years he was chaplain .of the 2nd V. 13. Cheshire Regiment, and was nearly completing his long- service time. At Raskelfe he w.is m,st populqr, his genial manner endearing him to all. Dr. Preston was all his life throngh,devotNlly attaehe:l t.) evangelical truth. He married Marv, the daughter of the late Mr. B. Darhyshire, J.P.. of Birkenhead, who with his two daughters survive him.

Fireman's Dinner. There was a pleasant gathering at the Cattle Mar- ket Hotel on Friday evening when the members of. the fire brigade held their annual dinner. Johns m, president of the National Spiritualist Union, gave an interesting address t ) the, Lyceum at the Spiritualist Hall, Rewsey-street, on Sunday afternoon, and in the evening dealt ably with a number of questions submitted by the audience, On Monday the annual New Year tea and social took place. A go 1(1 number of members and friends par.- to)k of tea, which was followed by an enjoyable pro- gramme of music, singing, phonograph " records,r dancing, and games. Mr. J. Collin manipulated phonograph, and a varied selection afforded consider- able pleasure. A quartet-party. consisting of Miss. Furness, Mr. F. Prvce-Jones, Mr. AV. Massey, and Mr.. H. Mr. Stout, who presided, expressed the- thanks of the Committee to all who had helped in any- way to make the social such a success. . The Thelwall Nuisance.

At a meeting of the Thelwall Parish Council on. Monday evening, Mr. N. Taylor presiding, the Clerk read a letter from Admiral Jones Parry in connection with "the Thelwall Nuisance," in which he stated that he and Mr. Linaker had examined the drains and: sewers in Weiste-lane. They were both of opinion that there was no necessity for the expenditure now being ,incurred.—Mr. Taylor said he. had • visited Weaste-lane: He found the old pipes, which were beini.`; taken .up, quite free from any trace of sewage,. the effluent was clear and there was no sediment and no smell. He had also thoroughly examined the drains, traps, &c., at Cuerden Hall, and was sure that Mr. Naylor's sewerage scheme was as perfect as modern sanitary engineering could make it.—Mr. Roe- buck said he had been told on good authority that a 15-inch pipe was sufficient to carry off the sewerage- of a town of 20,000 inhabitants, awl if that was true, he could not understand why they wanted an 18-inch pipe for Weaste-lane. —Mr. Hatton proposed the follow- ing resolution be sent to the County Council :--" The Th.elwall Parish Council protests against the expendi- ture now being incurred in Weaste-lane, on the ground that it is unnecessary, the Council being of opinion, from inspection, that the Weaste-lane sewer, now being taken up, is efficient and satisfact:)ry, and that the sewage from the houses which are connected. with this sewer cannot possibly have caused such a smell as is described by Mr. Naylor and his sup- porters."--The resolution was seconded by Mr. Houghton, and carried.
